## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accreditations are valid for 3 years. Any changes to the project or programme must be reflected in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in the next submission.
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The project responds to the coastal adaptation needs of Cuba due to slow onset events, including relative sea level rise and associated flooding arising from extreme weather events (hurricanes and extra-tropical storms). These threats are exacerbated by climate change, which is expected to increase in intensity and frequency, posing a threat to coastal settlements and communities. Projections show that if no intervention is made, by 2100, up to 21 coastal municipalities will be affected by sea-level rise, with an estimated 35% of the population exposed to coastal erosion and saline intrusion.

The project area has been identified as a priority for adaptation due its high vulnerability to climate change primarily in the form of coastal flooding and saline intrusion. 1,300 km of coastline, 24 communities, and 1,326,114 people will directly benefit from this project, which is expected to reduce exposure by up to 85% for these communities and their identified climate impacts.

Cuba’s coastal ecosystems are highly vulnerable due to its high vulnerability to climate change primarily in the form of coastal flooding and saline intrusion. 1,300 km of coastline, 24 communities, and 1,326,114 people will directly benefit from this project, which is expected to reduce exposure by up to 85% for these communities and their identified climate impacts.

Cuba’s coastal ecosystems are highly vulnerable due to its high vulnerability to climate change primarily in the form of coastal flooding and saline intrusion. 1,300 km of coastline, 24 communities, and 1,326,114 people will directly benefit from this project, which is expected to reduce exposure by up to 85% for these communities and their identified climate impacts.

Geomorphic processes in Cuba have led to an interconnected and diverse landscape in which different coastal ecosystems are interdependent. These include mangroves, seagrass beds, salt marshes, coral reefs, extensive low-lying and swampy coastal plains fringed by mangroves with coastal lagoons and estuaries adjacent to a wide range of terrestrial habitats including tropical rainforests, dry forests, savannas, wetlands in the mountains and marine environments, between fresh and saltwater sources, and between karstic processes and deep water.

The proposed project will enhance adaptive capacity by: holistically rehabilitating coastal land-seascapes, their interconnections, and ecosystem services; strengthening governance for coastal management through a participatory approach with mangroves and swamp forests and reducing anthropic pressures to marine coastal ecosystems, thus enhancing the resilience of these systems. The project will also strengthen the capacity of coastal communities and their adaptive capacities by: improving access to information on climate change (CC) and EBA training of managers will target national and local decision makers, teachers, economic sector leaders and academic institutions; strengthening the capacity of coastal communities through a participatory approach with mangroves and swamp forests and reducing anthropic pressures to marine coastal ecosystems, thus enhancing the resilience of these systems. Environmental information will be provided and integrated into existing information products to provide a fuller picture of coastal ecosystem services and their interconnections. The project will also strengthen the capacity of coastal communities through a participatory approach with mangroves and swamp forests and reducing anthropic pressures to marine coastal ecosystems, thus enhancing the resilience of these systems.

Project interventions will enable a new paradigm by positioning EBA directly into development strategies and actions in order to ensure that the ecosystem’s functional integrity is maintained. The project will provide direct support for and will serve as a baseline for the implementation of the Government of Cuba (GoC) Coastal Adaptation Strategy, which includes a set of coastal adaptation measures, tools, methodologies, and approaches which can be implemented elsewhere. Through its interventions, the project proposes direct and indirect benefits to coastal communities in terms of improved livelihoods, increased resilience, and enabling a transformation in how the GoC will manage climate change impacts on its coast and generate information used to inform similar applications in the Caribbean region.

[1] Stewart S., Kershaw F. (eds.) Framing the flow: Innovative Approaches to Understand, Protect and Value Ecosystem Services across Linked Habitats. UNEP World Conservation Monitoring Centre, Cambridge, UK

# B.1 Climate change and context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## Climate change impacts and threats

The Caribbean islands are vulnerable to climate change in the Caribbean, given its path of frequent tropical storms*, and the long narrow configuration of the country is such that no part of the country is very far from the sea (over 57% of the population lives in coastal municipalities).

### Coastal erosion and flooding

Coastal areas and low-lying settlements are also extremely vulnerable to climate change (CC) due to increased storm surges and rising sea levels, resulting in increased coastal flooding caused by extreme meteorological phenomena. The frequency of tropical cyclones has increased over the past 30 years. The number of hurricanes per year in this country has been affected by 12 hurricanes *which have been intense* (category 4 or 5; Figure B.7), the highest rate is in the Caribbean basin, and the region has experienced an increase in tropical cyclone activity since the mid-Atlantic region since 1998.

### Extreme weather events

The frequency of extreme weather events over these decades have also seen an increase in the occurrence of moderate and strong floods as a result of tropical cyclones and of extratropical systems, with extratropical cyclones being associated with increased precipitation. The Caribbean islands are also vulnerable to sea-level rise due to global warming, which is expected for the SLP mean sea level will increase by 0.29m by the year 2050 and between 0.22m and 0.60m by the year 2100* (modelled projections; see Table B.3 and Figure B.8). (Table B.2 Figure B.7). Continuing precipitation increases will lead to SLP flooding of up to 19.35cm by the year 2100 (Figure B.8).

### Sea-level rise

The projected increase in sea level is expected to be higher than the global average, with a range from 46 cm (CC + normal conditions) can be expected by the year 2050* (Passably Study Section 2.4.2). 

### Climate change and coastal erosion

There has been an increase in the frequency of storms, and new research that leads to a *increase wave heights in the Caribbean*. Under this scenario, storm waves will increase in height by up to 18% by the year 2050 (Figure B.9).

### CMIP5 projections

CMIP5* projections indicate that by 2050, mean annual temperature in Cuba will be by a median estimate of 1.0°C above current levels and by 2100 it is expected to increase by up to 3.4°C (RCP8.5). The number of days with temperatures exceeding 35°C will increase from the current average of 6-7 days per year to between 9 and 20 days (RCP8.5). Associated increases in potential evapotranspiration will further lead to more frequent severe droughts.

### Cuban coastal ecosystems

Cuban coastal seascape and landscapes are a succession of ecosystems that have covered over current climate change, with the exception of mangroves and coral reefs. The presence of mangroves, seagrass beds, meadows, beaches, coastal mangroves and forest or grassland swamps represents an equilibrium that confers resilience to these systems in the face of extreme events. However, the vulnerability of Cuban coastal ecosystems to extreme events and SLR is being undermined by both climate change effects (increased extreme weather events) and human activities such as urbanization, deforestation, overfishing and pollution. Mangroves have further suffered high levels of degradation affecting their ability to colonize new areas, reduce wave energy and provide habitat for fish species. The loss of mangrove ecosystems has been attributed to climate change and degradation that have been attributed to mangroves and sea grass degradation (including the alteration of hydrological conditions, increased sedimentation, pollution, overfishing and coastal development; see Figure 2), climate-related increases in surface water temperature and increased impacts of hurricanes*.

**B**

## Coastal flooding as a combination of high rainfall, high sea levels and storm surges has been identified as one of the primary climate change related threats to Cuba. Trends in the frequency of coastal floods during the period 1958-2013 show an increase in the number of days with coastal flooding for all regions except for the northern coastlines and strong rivers, regardless of the meteorological events that generate them (F3 Section 1.3). Specific factors contributing to this trend include: increased frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones, increased roughness and coastal vegetation coverage and conditions, with the coastal regions of La Colonia- Surgidero de Batabanó being particularly vulnerable.

### Hurricanes have also extensively damaged infrastructure. Hurricane Matthew, which crossed the eastern end of Cuba in October 2016, caused extensive damage to buildings, roads, bridges, and other infrastructure. It was one of the most devastating hurricanes to hit the island in the last decade, only behind the (2008) and Sandy (2012), with economic losses estimated at US$3 billion.

#### Saline intrusion

**Saline intrusion into aquifers is the most common and extensive cause of freshwater degradation in Cuba's coastal zones. Most of these aquifers, located near and beneath the northern and southern coasts, are open to the sea. The salinity of seawater can be as high as 35% (350 g/l), which is too high for human consumption and agriculture. It is estimated that by 2018 this will approach 644,000 km² in the case of projected interventions are already affected by saline intrusion.**

**Drinking water has been identified among the main risks for all Caribbean states, including Cuba. The most vulnerable areas are those located on the northern coast and in the period 1961-2013, rainfall was below normal in the majority of cases (Núñez et al., 2015).** Severe droughts have been increasing in eastern Cuba and are projected to increase in the future (Figures 33-34). Future projections indicate that the frequency of dry spells will increase by up to 70% between now and 2060, with an average reduction in relative humidity between 2% and 6% between 2030 and 2070, respectively (F5 Section 1.4). This is expected to have a significant impact on the agricultural sector, particularly during winter and dry seasons rainfall. This situation adds an increased pressure on the aquifers, which cannot be filled by rainwater.

### Vulnerability Southern Coast of Cuba, project target site

**The vulnerability assessment was conducted through extensive research led by The Ministry of Science, Technology and Environment (CITMA), the Environmental Agency (AMA) and the Scientific Institute for Water Resources (INR). It is based on the Cuban National Climate Change Strategy (2015-2030) and the National Environmental Strategy (NES) and its State Plan for Facing Climate Change (“Tarea Viva”, 2017) which outlined coastal vulnerability in Cuba.**

**A research-based CC vulnerability ranking (high, medium, and low) was designed considering a combination of factors such as: exposure to climate change impacts; sensitivity to these impacts; the capacity of the system to adapt; and vulnerability to sea-level rise and associated events is higher in the country's low-lying coasts.** The research also identified the most vulnerable areas (F5 Section 1.4). These are located on the southern coast of Cuba, particularly in the municipalities of Mayarí, Guantánamo, Caimanera, Jiguaní, and Playa Larga.

### Summa et Summa (2013) Macroregiones: Perfiles y contradistintos de la zona marino-costera de Cuba: estado actual y perspectivas ante el cambio climático 2150. Editorial Academia, La Habana

## Targeted Coastal Stretches: Tramo Y is Stretch 1 and Tramo Z is Stretch 2

### Southern Coastal Ecosystems

- **Figure 1. Targeted Coastal Stretches: Tramo Y is Stretch 1 and Tramo Z is Stretch 2**

#### Coastal ecosystems in the targeted coastal stretches are characterized mainly by low, swampy and mangrove-influenced areas, with extensive intertidal mudflats and seagrass beds, sandy beaches, rocky shores, coral reefs, keys and coral reef islands. In these areas mangroves and seagrasses could potentially act as protective barriers against storm surges, winds, waves and erosion. The resilience of coastal ecosystems is influenced by the sediment supply, water quality and connectivity. Coastal ecosystems can keep up with rising seas depending on sediment budgets, frequency of disturbances, connectivity, and ecosystem function.

- **Figure 2. Coastal Ecosystems and their Complex Interconnections**

#### There are numerous reported functional relationships between coastal and marine ecosystems, including sediment budgets, nutrient cycling, carbon sequestration, water quality, and biodiversity. These interactions are complex and dynamic. Freshwater inflow is favored by swamps forests reducing salinity intrusion risk and organic matter entering the coastal zone. The resilience of coastal ecosystems to climate change depends on their ability to adapt and connect; and connection is required to increase these ecosystems resilient to a changing climate and strengthening their protection against hazards.

#### Coastal ecosystems and their complex interconnections provide a variety of services for communities (Figure 2), including food, medicine, timber, recreation, cultural heritage, and coastal protection. These services are provided by healthy ecosystems, functional connections and when adequately integrated into land/marine planning policies[1-3].

## Integrated Coastal Ecosystems (Functional Coastal Landscapes) [Source: UNEP-WCMC, 2019]

Figure 2 summarizes the climate change rationale for the project, demonstrating how both observed historical and projected changes in climate lead to impacts on erosion, flooding and freshwater flows, which increase the risks of coastal vulnerability. The project will implement a suite of EBA solutions (mangrove regeneration and improved freshwater flows) that will lead to outcomes (coastal stability, protection and reduced salinity intrusion) which reduce environmental and social vulnerabilities to climate change impacts.

### Environmental and Social Conditions

- **Current conditions**
  - Coastal erosion
  - Flooding
  - Salinity intrusion
  - Reduced biodiversity
  - Increased vulnerability of coastal communities

### Climate Change Impacts (Baseline)

#### Current Climate Conditions
- Sea level rise
- Temperature increase
- More frequent extreme weather events

#### Future Projected Climate
- Continued sea level rise
- Increased frequency and intensity of storms
- Higher temperatures
- More frequent droughts

### EBA Solutions

1. **Mangrove regeneration**
   - Reduce coastal erosion
   - Improve water quality
   - Provide habitat for marine species

2. **Improved freshwater flows**
   - Reduce salinity intrusion
   - Enhance ecosystem resilience

### Project Outcomes

- Coastal stability
- Protection of coastal habitats
- Reduced vulnerability to climate change impacts

## Climate Impacts, Vulnerabilities, Risks and Reduction through EBA Solutions and Project Outcomes

The project will implement actions along Cuba's Southern Coast that have been selected due to their high vulnerability to climate change (open aquifers, low lying coastal plain, degraded ecosystems and concentration of settlements), particularly in the face of sea level rise.

### Targeted Shores
- Covers approximately 89,520 hectares of mangroves (representing 16.81% of the country's mangrove area)
- The project will regenerate approximately 37,400 ha of mangroves and improve 52,120 ha of degraded mangroves
- This intervention will turn to improving 2,287 ha of seagrass and 134 km of coral reefs and their respective protective functions

31. There is a risk of reef crest degradation (20), which in turn could cause significant wave damage in both mangroves and sea grasses reducing further their ability to offer protection against the effects of CC on the coast of Batán.
32. Restoration of degraded red mangroves (Rhizophora mangle) strips along the coastal edges, in stretches 1 and 2, is recommended as a priority action. The restoration should be carried out by means of planting seedlings in these areas, expressing areas dominated by black or white mangroves, which are less tolerant to saline water conditions. In addition, it is necessary to carry out an assessment of the potential for coastal erosion, which has originated the consequent loss of infrastructure and reduced livelihood opportunities (both fisheries and agriculture).
Coastal Stretch 1: La Colonia – Surgidero de Batán (271 km²; 13.520 ha)
The stretch is comprised by the municipalities of La Colonia, San Juan y Martínez, San Luis, Pinar del Río, Constancia del Sur, Los Palacios, San Cristóbal, Candelaria, Artemisa, and Bayamo. The main localities to intervene along this stretch include (1) La Colonia (in the municipality of La Colonia in Pinar del Rio Province), (2) Beach Capitán in Artemisa province; and, (3) Surgidero Batán(4) in Mayabeque province.
The vulnerability assessment concluded that, by 2100, 5 communities in this stretch could disappear due to SLR. Erosion is the main threat for these communities, which are located on the coast of La Colonia municipality. The measures needed be to take to reduce the impact:
- There should be a restoration and rehabilitation project of ecosystems degradation related to changes in land use, pollution past logging, grey infrastructure and inappropriate measures of coastal protection in the past, urbanization.
The vulnerability assessment concluded that, by 2100, 5 communities in this stretch could disappear due to SLR. Erosion is the main threat for these communities, which are located on the coast of La Colonia municipality. The measures needed be to take to reduce the impact:
- There should be a restoration and rehabilitation project of ecosystems degradation related to changes in land use, pollution past logging, grey infrastructure and inappropriate measures of coastal protection in the past, urbanization.
The impact of saline intrusion into the koralic aquifer is particularly troubling along this coastal stretch with important implications for water supply. The presence of mangroves has a positive effect on the quality of water in coastal communities and agriculture; it also is an important source of fresh water to the capital. Havana. To address the problem, there should be a restoration project of degraded red mangroves strips along the coast (2015-2030) at a cost of 4.7 million CUC built in 1991 aiming to accumulate runoff fresh water to the hat the infiltration of saline water in the stretch. The project has been carried out by means of planting seedlings in these areas, expressing areas dominated by black or white mangroves, which are less tolerant to saline water conditions. In addition, it is necessary to carry out an assessment of the potential for coastal erosion, which has originated the consequent loss of infrastructure and reduced livelihood opportunities (both fisheries and agriculture).
Coastal Stretch 2: Playa Larga – Playa de la Herradura (105 km²; 5.300 ha)
This stretch is comprised by provinces (Ciego de Ávila, Camagüey, Las Tunas and Granma) and 11 municipalities (Ciego de Ávila: Ciego de Ávila, Jiguaní, Remedios, Santa Clara; Camagüey: Camagüey, Guantánamo, Jibacoa, Manicaragua, Playa Larga y Marielitos); The main localities to intervene along this stretch include (1) Jiguaní in Ciego de Ávila Province, (2) Playa Larga in Camagüey province; and, (3) Playa de la Herradura in Granma province.

Mangrove vegetation is highly degraded. Wetlands in the prairie marshes have begun to dry due to a combination of climate drivers and land use change.
Coastal areas of the coastal area are located within external coastal wetlands dominated by mangroves, swamp grasslands and swamp forest.

The GCF’s approach is to enhance sustainable use of ocean biodiversity of global importance through an expanded, strengthened and integrated approach to marine protected area (MPA) management.

It mainly focused on biodiversity objectives. Preventing and controlling alien species were not an objective.

This experience will be scaled up directly or indirectly by other projects, as well as for the development of national policies and procedures. The project has been designed with a baseline estimate of the coastal resilience.

### Limitations on an EBA approach are many and varied

Limitations on an EBA approach are many and varied, including lack of capacity to implement EBA in a cost-effective manner; limited understanding of ecosystem connectivity and functioning; and insufficient data for decision-making. The project did not consider these limitations when developing the coastal resilience.

# UNIDISPERSALU: Building coastal resilience in Cuba through natural solutions

*Being implemented in a northern central coastal municipalities (Villa Clara, Sancti Spiritus, Ciego de Ávila & Camagüey) focusing on the protection of coastal communities and their livelihoods against climate change impacts.*

The project works to enhance local Disaster Risk Management Centers in an effort to integrate natural solutions into disaster risk management strategies. It will also support the development of a regional network of territories experimenting with limited nature-based solutions for local populations.

### Barriers to Action

1. Limited knowledge in vulnerable communities of CC drivers/impacts and adaptation options.
2. Lack of awareness among coastal communities about the characteristics of coastal and marine ecosystems, trends in climatic conditions, and the magnitude and nature of climate change impacts on these systems.
3. Limited understanding on CC adaptation. Climate information has mostly been used for disasters preparedness quite successfully but not yet as a tool to inform adaptation planning and implementation at local level.
4. Knowledge has yet evolved into actions that bridge sector gaps locally particularly in Technical information on CC adaptation is still lacking, and there are no clear guidelines or tools available to help communities understand how to adapt to climate change.

### Community Consultations

1. Community consultations indicate that communities perceive climate change as a future issue instead of an ongoing process.
2. There has been limited understanding of the range of adaptation options that exist for achieving sustainable resilience to CC, if a situation which, if unaddressed, will result in loss of livelihoods and assets.

### Actions at Local Level

1. Focus on preparation, attendance and reconstruction to individual impact events and not on long-term planning.
2. Community feel adaptation as a reactive strategy to temporal extreme events during which information flows top down from the government to communities.
3. There is limited knowledge among community members about the range of adaptation options that exist for achieving sustainable resilience to CC.

### Conclusion

1. Besides, a limited number of local communities have first-hand practical knowledge of the range of adaptation options that exist for achieving sustainable resilience to CC, if a situation which, if unaddressed, will result in loss of livelihoods and assets.

**B**

overwhelmingly the most cost-effective forms of adaptation such as hard infrastructure that are difficult to maintain and do not fully address the range of impacts these communities will be facing. This was made particularly evident during natural disasters, when many of these communities were unable to cope with the scale of damage caused by storm surges or saline intrusion without considering that these would generate further stress to vulnerable ecosystems currently providing critical services.

Barrier 2: Limited expertise with effective and sustainable implementation of EBA
- K-T barriers are often not addressed in national strategies for ecosystem management, sustainable land use, biodiversity and conservation, but there is a generalized lack of knowledge and practical understanding on how to implement these approaches. This is particularly true when it comes to the development of adaptation strategy that faces a functional coastal landscape as is required for EBA.
- The lack of capacity to effectively monitor and evaluate ecosystem services and the associated resources required for effective EBA including the monitoring of intertidal ecosystems services and the impact of coastal management on these, is another barrier. This is particularly true in developing countries where the use of Remote Sensing (RS) and Geographic Information Systems (GIS) is currently not fully to capture and value the hydrological needs required for coastal ecosystems to deliver ecosystem services. The lack of capacity to effectively monitor and evaluate EBA also means that many of these actions are having on coastal marine systems and integrating them within a wider scope of coastal resilience.
- This lack of capacity is further compounded by the fact that there is currently no international agreement or framework that can make room for any inputs to EBA. EBA approaches and interventions are more successful when landscapes and ecosystems are managed as an integrated whole (Figure 2).

Barrier 3: Physical barriers that reduce natural ecosystem response capacities
- The conversion of coastal wetlands, mangroves and coral reefs into agricultural land, pastures and settlements changing the natural dynamics, particularly those related with sediment trapping and water flow. There are infrastructure barriers such as dams, levees, dikes and other structures that can also have a significant impact on the hydrological regime within the ecosystems altering the natural dynamics and flows as well as infrastructure along the shoreline and coastal zone that can reduce the ability of these ecosystems to respond to climate change (Figure 65, FS). Land conversion, deforestation, increased sedimentation, pollution, changes on freshwater flow regimes, overfishing and other human activities are also reducing the resilience of coastal wetlands, mangroves and coral reefs systems (Feasibility Study Section 2.3). Upon field visits the construction of hard infrastructure along vulnerable coastlines was evident within the context of national physical planning (FS Section 6.2).
- But in general, there is a lack of coordination between Coastal Conservation Areas (CCA) and regulations
- AMCOS: governmental coordination has been progressively strengthened in recent years; there is still an entrenched tendency for institutional actors -environment, social-production- to work in silos, and for taking decisions at the national level. This means that coastal management actions are often implemented in a fragmented way where, there is an expectation to follow national sectoral directives adopted at the national level: In addition, there is a lack of coordination between different levels of government (national, provincial and municipal) which results into a limited capacity to take decisions locally, and such decisions are often decided by one sector alone (a decision-making process that can be counterproductive in terms of coastal resilience). This also hinders the ability for adaptation and natural resources management in decision-making at the territorial level. While a legal framework has been established in some countries, there is still a lack of capacity to effectively implement it.
- This is also aggravated as highly relevant information on ecosystems management (ecological flow of water for example) are not always available or accessible.

Barrier 5: Limited access and/or availability of finance for technical equipment to mobilize large scale adaptation interventions
- Climate change adaptation has been hindered by limited availability of funds for investment. Those baseline studies that have been carried out in the past have highlighted the need for additional funding, particularly in the case of access to technical and monitoring equipment that would allow a successful implementation of EBA. The lack of capacity to effectively monitor and evaluate ecosystem services and the impact of coastal management on these is another barrier. This results in higher costs and difficulties in acquiring and importing equipment and technology from international markets. In addition, there are limited national capacities for developing technical capacities for natural environmental management are high, its ability to transform these actions and knowledge into effective adaptation interventions is low. There is a need for additional funding that will allow access to and acquisition of the necessary equipment and technology that will allow for correct modeling, measuring and evaluation of coastal resilience and solutions.

### B

**60.** Hence, the mobilization of sustainable solutions that favor national capacity is crucial, particularly as hard infrastructure solutions have demonstrated to provide partial results and demand extensive amount of investment over a long period of time. The current crisis has also highlighted the need for countries to diversify their budget and is resorting to domestic bond issuance to finance the budget deficit that has arisen as a result of the current crisis. This is an important step in building national capacity, particularly in countries with limited access to international capital markets (which do not have a private banking sector) and managing the fall-out from the implementation of COVID-19 management measures. The GCF is already working on this issue and has shown that it can be done within the existing framework of the Fund, which already exists and has shown (See Economic Analysis and FS 5.3.6.) overtime to require reduced operation and maintenance costs.

**61.** While international cooperation projects in the past have been successful in achieving results, these have often been limited by the fact that they are not integrated into national development plans. The current crisis and several causes for the above failure includes limited appreciation at the sectoral level, because of the wrong perception of the role of climate change adaptation in the context of COVID-19 response. This is a key barrier that needs to be addressed through this project to ensure that interventions are not isolated investments but form part of national development plans.

**62.** **B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)**

**63.** Coastal ecosystems have the potential to impact CC coastal areas due to their sensitivity with the increase intensity and frequency of extreme weather events such as storm surges and flooding of coastal areas. Coastal ecosystems are also more vulnerable to sea level rise (SLR) and other impacts associated with SLR, such as increased exposure to CC and more likely to be affected by extreme weather events because of their low elevation, target fat topography, and high population density.

**64.** The diverse coastal ecosystems in these areas and their complex interconnections if restored can provide a variety of ecosystem services that can help reduce the impacts of climate change on coastal communities. Coastal ecosystems act as protective barriers against storm surges reducing coastal erosion, flood and salt intrusion while also providing other benefits such as carbon sequestration, water purification, which helps to improve water retention, create equilibrium dynamics and coastal stability in target coastlines.

**65.** In the context of CC adaptation, there are several key barriers that need to be addressed. These include EBA, knowledge, tools and capacities as identified in the barrier section have been lacking to enable its deployment at scale. The project will address these barriers by providing a set of enabling conditions which both implement and manage EBA measures through actionable measures to enhance coastal resilience beyond traditional approaches. This will be done by identifying and scaling up existing successful climate change solutions that have proven to be effective in these areas.

**66.** The project will focus on the following three sub-objectives: If coastal ecosystems along Cuba's southern coastline and their complex interconnections are rehabilitated and adaptive capacity in stakeholders is increased, then this will lead to a reduction of vulnerability and increase resilience of coastal communities in low lying coastal settlements because coastal ecosystem services can be maintained and restored. This will also result in the creation of new opportunities for sustainable livelihoods as well as the integration (mainstreaming) of EBA into development planning mechanisms, processes and budgets. and prioritization options including streamlining EBA within local governance planning frameworks budgets as foreseen in the GCC 2019 Constitution, which bestows greater responsibility to local actors in development and financing of coastal resilience projects.

This project, through both outputs, will allow communities to adapt proactively and enhance coastal resiliency by focusing on ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) and integrated coastal zone management. The project will be implemented through information products that will be enhanced and developed through the project making use of existing scientific knowledge and data. The project will also make use of the latest science, technology, innovation, and data analysis that will be developed through the project is considered unique for GCC and will work at providing a new approach to coastal resilience in the region.

Data provided will be used to adjust the priorities of the local CC-impact/coastal drivers and to determine more appropriate adaptation options.

The project's approach follows the recommendation by IPCC to integrate the multiple coastal zones uses in planning and management. The project will focus on three main coastal zone types: mangrove, coral reef, and sandy beaches. These stressors are considered feasible, cost-effective and highly scalable. Combined with the advantages of using local knowledge and traditional practices, this approach is expected to be more effective than a top-down approach.

The project will support the development of tools and methodologies for integrated coastal zone management in partnership with local communities, cultures, and knowledge. The project will provide the support needed to shift the paradigm from reactive to proactive adaptation by focusing on ecosystem-based approaches that ensure long-term resilience and sustained application of EBA while integrating local communities and enabling inter-sector coastal planning.

**Cuba: Coastal Resilience to CC through EBA “Mi Costa”**

### Figure 3: Theory of Change and its baseline assumptions

#### B.3 Project/programme description (max. 200 words, approximately 4 pages)

The proposed project's objective (outcome) is to strengthen coastal resilience to climate change along 1,700 km of coastline in Cuba. The project will focus on three main coastal zone types: mangrove, coral reef, and sandy beaches. It will do so through an EBA approach, restoring ecosystems nexus and functionalities, and by strengthening adaptive capacity at the local level.

The project will support the development of tools and methodologies for integrated coastal zone management in partnership with local communities, cultures, and knowledge. The project will provide the support needed to shift the paradigm from reactive to proactive adaptation by focusing on ecosystem-based approaches that ensure long-term resilience and sustained application of EBA while integrating local communities and enabling inter-sector coastal planning.

The project's 10-year objective will be calibrated with information originated by two monitoring mechanisms: i) environmental indicators, and ii) social indicators. The project's 5-year objective will be calibrated with information originated by the project in its entirety will be managed and coordinated by AMA with the support of national actors that will be hired or existing ones.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 14 OF 67

## Sub-activity 3.1: Identify and prioritize coastal wetlands for restoration and rehabilitation

### 74. Outline of approach on rehabilitating coastal wetlands structure and functionalities along the water-marginal coastland as presented in Figure 2b about actions projected impacts along coastal zones consequence of relative sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity, and changes in salinity due to freshwater discharge derived from climate change. Through the interactions of the four activities in this output, the project will effectively implement a comprehensive approach for restoring and rehabilitating coastal wetlands structure and functionalities to reduce such impacts. The proposed activities are thus aimed at strengthening coastal wetlands and marine ecosystems' capacity to cope with flooding, extreme climates, and salinity by restoring their hydrological system resilience (including communities) to the identified climate change impacts. This project follows the recommendations of the GCF-2018/039/001 Project Design Document for Coastal Wetland Restoration and rehabilitation by coastal communities by humans.

### 75. The selection criteria and methods to be applied have been based on the sound understanding and relevance of ecosystems' functional connectivity and environmental continuum 'their basin-coast zone-continental shelf system'. This is in line with the GCF-2018/039/001 Project Design Document for Coastal Wetland Restoration and rehabilitation by coastal communities by humans. Approaches to reducing total stressors are considered feasible, cost-effective and highly scalable within the context of the project's geographical area. The proposed activities will be implemented in close collaboration with local communities as well as national best practices in natural resource management.

### 76. Monitoring and evaluation will focus on assessing ecosystems' responses (including their health, connectivity and reduced pressures) to rehabilitation conditions as indicators of increased coastal resilience* and will be conducted through the use of a combination of remote sensing and ground-based monitoring tools. Indicators of infrastructure measures are not feasible (Feasibility Study Section 5.6 and 6.1).

### 77. Action areas for restoration and rehabilitation of coastal wetlands structure and functionalities along the water-marginal coastland as presented in Figure 2b about actions projected impacts along coastal zones consequence of relative sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity, and changes in salinity due to freshwater discharge derived from climate change.

#### Sub-activity 3.1.1: Identify and prioritize coastal wetlands for restoration and rehabilitation

### 78. This sub-activity focuses on initial actions for the rehabilitation of coastal wetland functions by reestablishing the hydrological processes within coastal ecosystems. This will include the clearing of existing water channels and removing barriers to improve the flow of freshwater, restoring mangrove forests, seagrass beds, and other habitats to improve their ecosystem's coping capacity to flooding, extreme climates, and salinity intrusion by restoring water flow and improving connectivity between wetlands and rivers. The proposed actions are aligned with GCF funds through Activity 1.4 will complement these actions. Rehabilitation actions will be implemented by 6 Coastal Wetland Restoration and rehabilitation by coastal communities by humans (CWR) units in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR) and the National Commission for Water Management (CNAM). The CWR units will use the equipment purchased through GCF funds (See FS Section 3.1.2).

### 79. This sub-activity will be implemented by a team of experts who will verify baseline conditions and monitor on the effectiveness of the restoration actions (habitat water flow, new plantings, increase in folage etc.), which is a key indicator for coastal wetland rehabilitation. The monitoring will also include the verification that the protection is rehabilitated. Experts from the Institute of Ecology and Systems Sciences (IES) and IHR institute of Tropical Geography and Environment (IGET) will be responsible for the implementation of this sub-activity in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MENR).

### 80. Action areas for restoration and rehabilitation of coastal wetlands structure and functionalities along the water-marginal coastland as presented in Figure 2b about actions projected impacts along coastal zones consequence of relative sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity, and changes in salinity due to freshwater discharge derived from climate change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 15 OF 67

## Coastal ecosystem conditions: baseline validation and rehabilitation progress will be assessed in situ through transects and permanent plots and complemented with drone image and remote sensing analyses to better gauge the effectiveness of restoration actions, including their impact on species dominance and distribution, soil conditions and microclimates, and major zoological groups. Water salinity, quality, temperature, dissolved oxygen, pH, turbidity, sedimentation rates, and other water quality parameters will be monitored at baseline and during project implementation to assess changes in water health and functionalities for resilience**12**and will provide information to assess the effectiveness of the restoration actions on improving water quality. The same monitoring will also be conducted at the end of the project and during the project’s lifetime.

## Additional monitoring will result in an updated forest profile and basic structure as well as an updated forest landuse map that will include indicators on hydrology within and across the ecosystems, health and functionalities for resilience**12**, and other relevant information to assess the effectiveness of the restoration actions. The GCF will provide funding for the development of a monitoring plan and the capacity to develop these products.

### Sub activity 1.1.2 Restore the ecological flow of freshwater towards mangrove ecosystems through cleaning up existing water channels and building small scale low impact infrastructure to facilitate the laminar flow of water during dry periods.

## Forestry enterprises will perform the rehabilitation of the coastal wetland hydrology in each target site to ensure the establishment of a continuous flow of fresh water from the rivers into the mangroves, which will favor the health and stability of coastal ecosystems including:

- Restoring the ecological flow through the cleaning of existing water channels;
- The establishment of small drain channels (less than 50 cm wide) allow the ecological water flow in the case where there are no natural waterways. These drains will be built with a low impact infrastructure that will pass through the natural meandering and water flow areas to allow the laminar flow of water.
- The construction of small dams (less than 1 m high) to retain fresh water during rainy periods to mangrove areas will further facilitate the flow of water to wetlands.

### Sub activity 1.1.3 Forest enterprises in coastal wetland restoration projects will also perform activities for the cleaning and establishment of water channels, (mats-inlets with front boards and amphibious equipment for cleaning ditches that can be used as a temporary dam) to ensure the flow of fresh water from rivers into mangrove areas. These actions through the hiring of 6 forestry enterprises will develop the restoration actions in accordance with national legislation and GCF guidelines. The use of low impact infrastructure (e.g., mats-inlets, amphibious equipment for cleaning (panalari and co-farming) will further be used in removing infrastructure barriers along the coastline and the coastal wetlands. A monitoring plan has been developed on EAS to ensure that all interventions are carefully monitored per GCF and UNDP standard protocols.

### Sub activity 1.1.4 Invasion species management in select sites to reduce pressures on the coastal wetland and mangrove ecosystems

## Management of invasive species (ES) has been identified, through national research and baseline projects, to be managed at the project level as a priority action. The ESs are those that have already invaded large areas across the water resources, distinct natural water courses and contribute to swamping dry.

### Sub activity 1.2 Ecosystem restoration in coastal wetlands

- Mangroves and swamp forests: IES include Casuarina and Terminalia catappa. Other species commonly found in these ecosystems are Acacia mangium, Barringtonia acutangula, Bruguiera gymnorhiza, Rhizophora sp., and the Asian colubrine. Interventions have included the filling of individual trees followed by covering the base of the tree to avoid regeneration of regrowth at stump as suggested.

## B

### Activity 1.2 Mangrove and swamp forest rehabilitation in target sites through natural and assisted regeneration (sub-activities: 1.2.1, 1.2.2, 1.2.3)

This activity will result in the rehabilitation of ecosystem functionalities in 15,438 ha of degraded coastal wetlands with mangroves and swamp forests, including 70% of the total area of mangrove forest and 30% of the total area of grasslands.

This activity along with actions through Activity 1.1, will directly improve the coping capacity of coastal wetlands to face climate change impacts (by strengthening the resilience of ecosystems) and reduce vulnerability in areas where mangroves have been lost (R: mangals), climate extremes (by strengthening the coastal system green structure and recovering its functions).

Interventions for rehabilitation across ecosystems will be developed through local forest enterprises hired by AMA through a tender process, with the participation of the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MMAF) and national research (Section B.2). These include targeted ecosystem rehabilitation, reestablishing key species, restoring degraded areas, and strengthening coastal systems.

This activity takes into account the integrated nature of GCF and co-financing resources as critical inputs for action 1.2. This will be achieved through a combination of direct funding from GCF (sub-activities: 1.2.1) and co-adaptation while co-financing funds will implement sub-activities 1.2.2 & 1.2.3 in the form provided equipment. Rehabilitation activities will be carried out by local forest enterprises hired through a tender process, with the participation of MMAF and national research (Section B.2).

GCF resources will also be specifically used for the acquisition of required equipment and software/hardware (Sub-activity 1.2.1) that will be used in the restoration sites (Sub-activity 1.2.2-1.2.3) by forest enterprises and its protection through the implementation of EBA actions (Section B.4). The GCF includes the implementation of restoration actions as well as purchase of national insurance for the investment portfolio during rehabilitation until the first 5 years of project implementation against climatic or accidental events.

### Sub-activity 1.2.1 Acquire foregone and evaluation equipment for restoration in target sites (sub-activities: 1.2.1, 1.2.2, 1.2.3) (Section B.3.1.2)

This includes the purchase of agriculture equipment difficult to access in Cua Barra (Sub-activity 1.2.1), which will be used for the implementation of restoration investments (Sub-activity 1.2.1-2.3). This includes the establishment of nurseries and the planting of mangrove species, as well as the maintenance of coastal ecosystem interventions throughout the project’s full life time (30 years) while directly enhancing the resilience of ecosystems to climate change impacts.

Equipment will be acquired through GCF funds during the project's first and second year and will follow UNDP's procurement guidelines. The equipment will be used for the implementation of restoration actions, as well as the maintenance of coastal ecosystem interventions throughout the project’s full life time (30 years) while directly enhancing the resilience of ecosystems to climate change impacts.

Equipment to be acquired includes tractors, chainsaws, refrigerators, and other tools necessary for the mobilization of workers and for surveillance measures, sprinklers, water pumps, tools for restoration and surveillance, measuring and communication equipment.

# Sub-activity 1.2.3 Implementation of natural regeneration management measures in target sites

98. Forests rehabilitation of targeted mangroves and swamp forests will be based on their current degradation condition and the restoration potential of each site. The rehabilitation actions will include planting of mangrove seedlings in areas for mangrove and swamp forest restoration per project site. Actions for natural regeneration include the inclusion of native species in the planted seedlings, as well as the use of best practices to ensure that conditions allow trees to grow. In the case of swamp forests and grasslands, actions include the clearing of weeds as well as the management of invasive species. These actions will be implemented by forest enterprises that will make use of the equipment acquired in Sub-activity 1.2.1. The rehabilitation activities will also include the planting of mangrove seedlings to ensure the natural growth rate and the implementation of controlled extraction measures to reduce the concentration of documents.

99. Rehabilitation will be funded by GCF via financing (MINAG and CIFINAN) through the hiring of forest enterprises that will make use of the equipment acquired in Sub-activity 1.2.1. The rehabilitation activities will also include their maintenance. This includes the cost of labor, oversight, expert technical advice. All interventions will be managed following the project's ESAR national legislation and protocols for forest management.

# Sub-activity 1.2.3 Red mangrove and native species planting in target sites for forest rehabilitation along the shoreline boundaries of targeted coastal ecosystems

101. Mangrove restoration efforts will rehabilitate the protection strip that used to be dominated by red mangrove and other native species. The rehabilitation actions include the planting of mangrove seedlings, as well as the use of best practices to ensure that conditions allow trees to grow. In addition, these actions will also include the implementation of controlled extraction measures to reduce the concentration of documents.

102. Swamp forest rehabilitation actions will enhance native species diversity (often affected by illegal logging), with the planting of seedlings and the maintenance along the swamp forests that will in turn be monitored through Activity 1.1 Actions increase including increasing the number of propagules available for propagation and plant once grown. Actions to stimulate growth along the wetlands will also be implemented including the use of best practices to ensure that conditions allow trees to grow.

103. These actions will be implemented by forest enterprises that will make use of the equipment acquired in Sub-activity 1.2.1. The rehabilitation activities will also include their maintenance. This includes the cost of labor, oversight, expert technical advice. All interventions will be funded by the GCF via financing this sub activity through hiring of forestry enterprises, excluding the cost of labor and oversight. All interventions will be managed following the project's ESAR national legislation and protocols for forest management.

# Sub-activity 1.2.4 Implementing external risk management measures in target sites to ensure persistence of targeted coastal ecosystems

104. Actions under this sub activity will protect restoration investments from external risks (i.e., illegal logging and poaching, fire, etc.). These actions include the implementation of natural regeneration management measures within the project to ensure that coastal ecosystems have the opportunity to develop during their initial years of growth. The rehabilitation activities will also be implemented in areas specifically targeted for restoration with the support of the equipment acquired in Sub-activity 1.2.1. Cost of labor, oversight and expert technical advice will be covered by GCF via financing this sub activity through hiring of forestry enterprises.

105. Protective measures to the coastal ecosystems include:

* The implementation of fire control systems (including firebreaks and trails) along swamp grass lands and active training of fire rangers in forest areas. Protocols for these systems will build upon best practices generally used by forest enterprises.
* The use of controlled extraction measures to reduce the concentration of documents through the restored hydrology network of existing water channels.
* The implementation of controlled extraction measures (native species in swamp forests).
* Further actions include the establishment of fire rangers in project sites during the initial years of project implementation against climatic emergencies (extreme weather).

### Activity 1.4: Enhance water conduction systems along targeted watersheds to reduce freshwater salinity intrusion

#### Sub-activity 1.4.1: Remove obstacles along water channels and enable water conduction systems in target areas to allow the natural flow of freshwater into coastal ecosystems.

125. The implementation of the marine monitoring and surveillance systems will be funded by the GCF that will implement this sub-activity through financing provided by the NRH which will include building wells and permanent structures for the installation of solar energy, including the construction of a Knowledge Management Platform (Activity 2.2) created through this project. As stated in p.170, these sites will contribute to the monitoring equipment (See Section 6.E.) that will be employed for the monitoring itself.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.1: Remove obstacles along water channels and enable water conduction systems in target areas to allow the natural flow of freshwater into coastal ecosystems.

127. During the first 5 years of GCF intervention, this project will result in the reduction of salt intrusion by 13,329 ha. This is equivalent to a reduction of 40% of the total area affected by salinity intrusion and represents an important sustainable reduction of the salt wedge to improve freshwater availability in SRL 500 km² by reducing saline intrusion and improving water quality. The project will also contribute to the identification of catchment areas, seawater catchment and salt-wedge advancement/receding that will improve the management of coastal ecosystems and communities. In addition, this project will contribute to water management for adaptation in coastal ecosystems and communities. In strengthening the present hydrological network, it is expected that the freshwater resources will be better managed and used more efficiently.

129. GCF funds to be used in the purchase of monitoring systems will ensure that the required quantity and quality data are collected from the different sites where the project interventions will take place. This will allow for the installation of solar energy, including the construction of a Knowledge Management Platform (Activity 2.2) created through this project. As stated in p.170, these sites will contribute to the monitoring equipment (See Section 6.E.) that will be employed for the monitoring itself.

## B

### Sub-activity 4.3: Developing hydrological models to support hydrological processes and water management (identification of catchment areas, salt wedge advancement modeling and salinity intrusion) to better assess the impacts of SDR and climate change on coastal communities.

This sub-activity will focus on working with coastal communities located along climate-vulnerable coastlines, local governments, and national government agencies. The objective is to develop a set of hydrological models and water management measures while working to enhance local and national governance mechanisms within the framework of Tarea Vida. This will allow for the contribution and the correct packaging of information derived from Output 1 to foster the development of an integrated approach to coastal vulnerability assessment, risk reduction, and climate change adaptation and will create opportunities for inter-sector collaboration to ensure that EBA investments are appropriately targeted in order to maximize their effectiveness and sustainability while ensuring long-term sustainability.

A novel approach to this sub-activity will be sectoral and municipal regulatory frameworks strengthening specifically on EBA mainstreaming and approaches based on results derived (such as ecological flow calculation) from Output 1 and the development of a set of hydrological models. The use of these models in conjunction with the outputs will allow for the contribution and the correct packaging of information derived from Output 1 to foster the development of an integrated approach to coastal vulnerability assessment, risk reduction, and climate change adaptation and will create opportunities for inter-sector collaboration to ensure that EBA investments are appropriately targeted in order to maximize their effectiveness and sustainability while ensuring long-term sustainability.

Through enhanced understanding, awareness and capacity to identify and monitor coastal vulnerabilities to climate change and sea-level rise, the project will support the development of a set of hydrological models for adaptation funding solutions that take advantage of national best practices, ecologies (as demonstrated in Output 1) and local knowledge. The project will also ensure that these models are relevant and useful by engaging in continuous consultation from communities themselves to ensure that its material is relevant and useful.

AMA will be responsible for the development of the hydrological models with active involvement and support of local representatives. It will rely on the active involvement and support of municipal government and structures.

Activity 2.1.3 Develop a climate adaptation technical capacity building program for coastal communities and local stakeholders (government & economic sectors) to enable adaptation actions and capacities

### 124. Training on climate change vulnerability assessment and adaptation planning
This activity will be carried out in 7 provinces where the project is implemented, targeting 24 municipalities (2024 municipalities) to build understanding of CC impacts and vulnerabilities such as coastal flooding and sea level rise, and to develop capacity for adaptation actions. The training will focus on raising awareness and knowledge for adaptation actions and skills to strengthen the resilience of key stakeholders in these communities. This will be done through a series of workshops that will be held at the provincial level. The content derived from this project as seen in Output 1 to ensure their maintenance and replication. It will also allow provinces to develop their own training programs, which can be adapted for other areas within the province or locally relevant learning network.

### 125. Training on climate change vulnerability assessment
This activity will allow for the coordination of key actors in municipalities (economic, learning and government sectors) thus integrating valuable technical information derived through project monitoring and evaluation into a single training program that can be used to develop adaptation action plans (e.g., coastal adaptation water management, physical planning, adopted livelihoods). This will allow the curriculum to be relevant to local needs and priorities. The training will also include practical exercises on how to integrate climate change vulnerability assessment in existing development plans.

### 126. Training on climate change vulnerability assessment
Training under this activity will be targeted to 24 municipalities located within 7 provinces in the project's territory, which will be selected based on their level of exposure and vulnerability to CC impacts as well as priority areas where rehabilitation interventions (Output 1) are taking place.

### 127. Training on climate change adaptation planning
Training under this activity will be targeted to 30 key actors from each province (FSE Section 4.2.3.1 listed in each municipality that will be enhanced as well as through F SEs that will be created in each of the restoration coordination structures). The training will focus on developing a climate change adaptation action plan for each of these municipalities, based on the vulnerability assessment and adaptation planning workshops (Output 1) and the adaptation framework (considering they will be informed by local climate information products (Activity 2.2)). The training will also include practical exercises on how to integrate climate change adaptation into existing development plans.

### 128. Training on climate change adaptation planning
This activity will allow for the coordination of key actors in municipalities (economic, learning and government sectors) thus integrating valuable technical information derived through project monitoring and evaluation into a single training program that can be used to develop adaptation action plans (e.g., coastal adaptation water management, physical planning, adopted livelihoods). This will allow the curriculum to be relevant to local needs and priorities. The training will also include practical exercises on how to integrate climate change vulnerability assessment in existing development plans.

### 129. Training on climate change adaptation planning
Training under this activity will be targeted to 30 key actors from each province (FSE Section 4.2.3.1 listed in each municipality that will be enhanced as well as through F SEs that will be created in each of the restoration coordination structures) which have proven to be a local expression on coordination on issues such as disaster risk planning and management, coastal adaptation water management, physical planning, adopted livelihoods.

### 130. Training on climate change adaptation planning
This activity will allow for the coordination of key actors in municipalities (economic, learning and government sectors) thus integrating valuable technical information derived through project monitoring and evaluation into a single training program that can be used to develop adaptation action plans (e.g., coastal adaptation water management, physical planning, adopted livelihoods). This will allow the curriculum to be relevant to local needs and priorities. The training will also include practical exercises on how to integrate climate change vulnerability assessment in existing development plans.

### 131. Training on climate change adaptation planning
Training under this activity will be targeted to 30 key actors from each province (FSE Section 4.2.3.1 listed in each municipality that will be enhanced as well as through F SEs that will be created in each of the restoration coordination structures) which have proven to be a local expression on coordination on issues such as disaster risk planning and management, coastal adaptation water management, physical planning, adopted livelihoods.

existing C4C communities (expanding their physical and material capacity for training) and in developing 7 classrooms that will be established through GCF co-financing (municipal domain of the space) in the seven ecosystem intervention areas. ### Activity 2.1 Implement in 24 targeted municipalities and 7 Intervention sites a capacity building program through local structures (CECs) and accessed classroom rooms

146. Premises will be designed to meet the following: increasing capacity for training (25 people in CECs and 15 in each classroom), ensuring that classrooms are appropriate and acquire the tools necessary to manage climate change impacts, enhancing security; GCF funds will be used to enhance physical/infrastructure capacity within these Centres and class rooms (e.g. desks, chairs, computers, internet access, etc.), as well as for training materials, furniture, lighting, water taps, office furniture) and to guarantee their adequate operation (internet services).

147. Such premises will be managed by the CECs, which will be responsible for the administration of enhanced C4C classrooms and classrooms; payment of current accounts (energy, water, cleaning security services, etc.), as well as for the maintenance of these facilities.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3 Implement in 24 targeted municipalities and 7 Intervention sites a capacity building program through local structures (CECs) and accessed classroom rooms

149. Specialists from FLACSO-Cuba and international specialists will be hired (see § 335 and 194) to provide technical assistance for the implementation of this sub-activity, as well as to support the development of a training manual (Feasibility Study Section 6.2.1.3) transforming them into key community trainers on specific topics such as nature-based solutions, climate change adaptation, coastal management and integrated coastal planning at a local level. It should be noted that prior to this activity adaptation and EBA training will be provided.

### Activity 2.2 Integrate project (technical and community based)-derived information, from early warning systems and national datasets into a Knowledge Management Platform, to provide climate information products and services for the coastal communities in Cuba

150. In the case of enhanced C4C (Sub-activity 2.1.3), these will be operated by two specialists (environmental and social) who will be responsible for the development of training materials, as well as for providing technical assistance to newly created classrooms; these will be operated by one specialist. CTIMS will be responsible for their salaries and payment of current accounts (energy, water, cleaning security services, etc.), as well as for the maintenance of these facilities.

151. Ethnosed BSCs will be key for providing EBA awareness and training to communities; these Centers will be responsible for their salaries and payment of current accounts (energy, water, cleaning security services, etc.) as well as for the maintenance of these facilities. These centers will also provide technical assistance levels leveraging their role as knowledge brokers transforming them into crucial actors at a territorial level to coordinate climate change adaptation.

### Activity 2.2 Integrate project (technical and community based)-derived information, from early warning systems and national datasets into a Knowledge Management Platform, to provide climate information products and services for the coastal communities in Cuba

153. Climate information products will be developed by means of an integrated approach derived from the local ecosystems rehabilitation actions and hydrological dynamic restoration (Output 2) to integrate it into national databases through a Knowledge Management Platform (KMP). The KMP will be used as a tool to provide climate information products and services for Coastal Resilience Assessment (CRA) (Output 3); enhancing successful existing early warning systems (disasters and extreme events), EWS (early warning system) and CRA (coastal resilience assessment) through the development of indicators with data derived from common monitoring of local conditions. This work will be led by AMA through CICMAR and will be supported by national governments through international networks (See FS Table 25 for list of stakeholders and use of GCF funds).

154. Climate information products will be developed in a two-way information flow, flowing not only down from national governments to settlements but also up from the communities to the national government. This will ensure that climate information products are built upon transparent and clearly identified data that will serve as important decision-making tools for coastal communities. These products will be used by local authorities to assist local conditions and serve as an early warning for coastal capacity to adaptation responding to the various hazards particular to coastal communities (coastal flooding, saline intrusion, extreme weather).

**Page 28**

154. Through the involvement of key actors the project will favor the delivery of the core elements of a people-centered and end-to-end MAPP that already exists in Cuba. Through its environmental monitoring the project will also contribute to the development of an integrated coastal management system (ICMS) for the island, which will enhance disaster risk knowledge by incorporating ecosystem capacity to manage climate threats and better assess vulnerability and resilience. The ICMS will be based on a participatory approach involving all relevant actors who will be accessible to key decision makers within the EWS so that facilitating information through participatory processes will be possible. This will also contribute to the development of a national guide described in section 2.2.4. This will further be enhanced through the delivery of informational products to communities.

155. Capacity building and awareness raising activities will be conducted with key actors, including local communities becoming active and informed drivers of adaptation and local agents for climate action adaptation. Sub activity 2.2.3 integrates project and national databases and monitoring systems into a Knowledge Management Platform (KMP) for Coastal Adaptation.

156. This activity will create a Knowledge Management Platform for Coastal Adaptation to integrate and manage information on coastal resilience, vulnerability and risk in the context of climate change. The KMP will be developed by the National Center for Information Systems (CENIS), with support from the Cuban Meteorological Stations by INSMET, Environmental Health Units, Natural Resources Forestry Companies, Protected Areas, and other relevant actors. The KMP will provide a single point of access to information for governments, sectors and society to monitor the project's objectives and progress over time; as well as identify priorities and opportunities for adaptation and intervention. More importantly it will provide continuous information on coastal resilience in targeted coastlines.

157. This activity will also develop an EBA node that will be integrated into the KMP. The EBA node will host all investigation and projects on coastal adaptation. The node will receive and integrate: i) Indicators of climate vulnerability and risk; ii) Information from the national database; iii) Data from the project's investigations; iv) The KMPCA will support the incorporation of EBA indicators into the National Statistical System (SIN), to be used by the government for planning, monitoring and evaluation. The KMP will also provide a platform for the integration and the incorporation of EBA measures in sectorial planning instruments. Through the platform the project will assist in the development of an integrated coastal management system (ICMS) for Cuba. This will allow the inclusion of EBA measures in sectorial planning instruments. Through the platform the project will assist in the development of an integrated coastal management system (ICMS) for Cuba. This will allow the inclusion of EBA measures in sectorial planning instruments.

158. The inter-territorial networks (at country, municipal, provincial level and national levels) created through the project will be used to disseminate information on coastal adaptation and vulnerability and risk. These networks will recommend actions developed in close cooperation with the territories (popular councils and municipal councils) to the National Government. This will allow for a more effective dissemination of knowledge generated from the territories (co-impact of intervention and EBA as a general strategy but back to them and between them). The inter-territorial networks will also be used to disseminate information on coastal adaptation and knowledge generated by the Project leveraging national and locally relevant knowledge management tools and databases.

159. GCF funds will be used for the purchase of information technology equipment including software to facilitate communication among actors involved in coastal adaptation, as well as for the provision of training and expertise and technical support services as detailed in the project log frame to enable the integration of EBA measures into sectorial planning instruments. The cost of these activities (training and expertise) will be financed by the GCF under dedicated co-financing provided by GTMA (FS Table 25).

Sub activity 2.2.4 Translates information generated from the project into a national database (indicators for economic, environmental, climate change health and disaster vector quality as part of a community vulnerability assessment) and disseminates information derived from USAID monitoring systems and better assess coastal vulnerability and resilience.

The GCF will finance the purchase of information technology equipment including software to facilitate communication among actors involved in coastal adaptation. The cost of these activities (training and expertise) will be financed by the GCF under dedicated co-financing provided by GTMA (FS Table 25).

The SEN is coherent with the national statistical systems of the country, which intend to meet the information needs of the same users. It also provides a framework for the integration of EBA measures into sectorial planning instruments and social development, as well as responding to international subsector commitments (Resolution(s) / GOC-2016-473-EX08).
