**Page 29**
---

161. The implementation of the community monitoring system will complement existing EWS Systems and all serve as a mechanism to increase ownership and empowerment of communities to incorporate adaptation alternatives in their livelihoods. In addition, it will provide early warning information on changes in coastal conditions (e.g., shoreline receding rates, maximum flood benchmarks, mangroves survival rates, among others) it will help promote the development of local monitoring systems that can be used for decision making and planning purposes so they can gradually adapt and adjust their own actions based on their risk perception.

162. The implementation of the community monitoring system is expected to have a positive impact on the resilience of the coastal zone (Output 1) with the involvement of the community in the generation of information and knowledge of local conditions to be used for decision making purposes. The information generated by the communities’ monitoring system will partly feed the KMPCA (Activity 2.2.1) and will be shared with CBS (Activity 2.3). In addition, the information generated from the community monitoring systems will be used in the development of local adaptation plans.

163. The information collected for coastal monitoring workshops - as trainee communities on EBA monitoring- and for the procurement of basic equipment (cameras, recording equipment, etc.) which will remain within the communities’ control to ensure sustainability of the system. The data generated from these activities will be shared with site specific EBA monitoring (TBs Table 29). Data from the monitoring will be transformed into local information products that will be used for coastal management and decision making.

164. The implementation of the community monitoring system will allow communities to take an active part in the information generation process, which is currently done by experts. This will lead to a more accurate information derived from climate change projections, which in case of the targeted communities will result in significant changes in their livelihoods and coastal conditions. In addition, it will provide a platform for increasing active participants in the information products that will be developed thus promoting a new real change from a traditional reliance on experts to an active participation by local communities in the generation of climate change related data where they are active and inform agents on climate risk and participate in its management, hence addressing a key priority of the KMPCA.

Sub-activity 2.2.3 Create user driven climate and environmental information products as tools for EBA implementation

165. Through this activity the project will generate climate and environmental information products as tools for EBA implementation. These products will be generated through the following activities: (i) generation of local climate change projections; (ii) development of a coastal vulnerability assessment; (iii) development of a climate change adaptation strategy; (iv) development of an early warning system; (v) development of a monitoring and evaluation framework, EWS and thus enhancing access to CC. Centres for Environmental Studies (CEEs) will be responsible for the generation of these products. The information generated from this activity will be used in local information systems such as the coastal erosion index, the saline intrusion line, coastal flooding characteristics, the size of mangrove areas, among others. These information products will be updated annually based on the results of the monitoring and complemented with the existing monitoring network.

166. Information products will be based on local climate and oceanographic data and will be prepared locally, taking into account the specific needs of the communities. The KMPCA (Activity 2.2.1) will provide a framework for the management, EWS and thus enhancing access to CC. Centres for Environmental Studies (CEEs) will be responsible for the generation of these products. The information generated from this activity will be used in local information systems such as the coastal erosion index, the saline intrusion line, coastal flooding characteristics, the size of mangrove areas, among others. These information products will be updated annually based on the results of the monitoring and complemented with the existing monitoring network.

167. A Protocol for Coastal Resilience Assessment (PERC), following the conceptualization of the recently developed KMPCA, will be developed to assess coastal resilience in the context of climate change. The PERC will be integrated into the Terrestrial Statistical Information System (SITIS), which will in turn ensure the inclusion of the most recent information on coastal conditions and vulnerability. The SITIS will also provide a platform for development planning and metrics. The PERC will take into account 5 outputs: vulnerability, sensitivity, adaptability, resilience and capacity. The results from this activity will be used to inform the KMPCA (Activity 2.2.1) and made available at a territorial level through CTIMA provincial designation. This will allow coastal communities to have access to information on their own vulnerability and thus take appropriate actions.

Activity 2.3. Mainstream EBA approaches into regulatory and planning frameworks at the territorial and national levels, as well as in the development of local adaptation plans

168. Activity 2.3 will provide inputs for generating the legal frameworks and financial mechanisms required for the strengthening of coastal management systems (CEEs) and the mainstreaming of EBA approaches within these systems funded with GCF funds). This will be done through various mechanisms for addressing the capacity and information barriers that exist at different levels, including training workshops, technical assistance to municipalities and standardizing technical information for the development of national guidelines. The results of this activity will also contribute towards ensuring that there are constitutional changes that will empower municipal governments in financial and natural resource management.

189. By using these tools and methodologies including that can be easily applied and locally relevant, municipal authorities will be able to incorporate an EBA approaches in their own economic and planning instruments (including the preparation of medium-term development plans and budget allocation for coastal areas as a result of climate change impacts. Sub activity 2.3.1 Creates a network of legal advisor to develop a detailed analysis of the regulatory, legal and institutional framework in each municipality and provincial government to provide technical advice on how to integrate EBA into municipal and / or provincial economic plans through concrete actions and investments.

### B

170. Through the network of advisors, the project will directly address existing capacity gaps identified during the previous phase (2014-2016) in terms of knowledge and skills related to the development of enabling frameworks for integrating EBA as an adaptation and sustainable development strategy, identifying implementation options and developing action plans for coastal areas. The network of advisors will also provide technical tools and recommendations that will be provided as direct support of the legal network to the 24 municipalities involved in the project (including the preparation of medium-term development plans and budget allocation financing), providing them the space (GIC funds) and the equipment required to operate (GIC funds), as well as for the implementation of EBA.

171. A key result under this sub-activity will be the development of environmental management plans in all coastal municipalities, including the preparation of action plans for climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction.

### B. Implementation Arrangements

179\. This project will be executed in the Republic of Cuba under Article 13 of the Standard Basic Agreement (SBA) between UNDP and the Government of Cuba, signed by the two parties on May 10, 1975. The objectives and expected results of the project correspond to the priorities defined in the Country Programme Document (CPD).

180\. The project implementation will be regulated by the provisions of Resolution 12/2006 of the former Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Cuba (MINEC), dated May 25, 2006; and the Rules for Economic Cooperation that Cuba has established with UNDP, as well as the rules for the management of projects under the framework of the Public Private Investment (MINCEX), dated May 26, 2006, which states that the Rules for Economic Cooperation that Cuba has established with UNDP.

181\. The project will be implemented following UNDP's national implementation modality (NIM) - according to the terms and conditions of the SBA. The project will be executed by the Government of Cuba through its implementing partner (IP), which is the Ministry of Environment, Natural Resources and Tourism (MINATUR). MINATUR will act as the executing agency (EA) for this project.

182\. The project will be implemented in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the SBA. All references in the SBA to "Executing Agency" shall be deemed to refer to "Implementing Partner".

183\. The Government of Cuba is responsible for ensuring that the implementing partner complies with all laws required to implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies and procedures (P&P), work plans, and other relevant guidelines. The implementing partner will also be responsible for ensuring that social safeguards, and other performance standards.

184\. AMA will be accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including monitoring and evaluation of project implementation, reporting on progress against the approved work plan, and providing technical assistance to the National Project Director to carry out the strategic direction of the project and oversee its operational and technical execution. The National Project Director will provide guidance to MINATUR in the use of these ICMAT and CITMA's specialists based in the project involved provinces and municipalities. AMA will ensure that UNDP policies are followed, including those related to the recovery of direct project costs relating to GCF funded projects.

185\. The management arrangements for this project are summarized in Figure 4.

## Figure 4. Organization Structure

The Inter-Ministerial Coordination Committee (ICC) for the GCF in Cuba, the First Deputy Minister of CTIMA was designated as the ICC Chairperson. The ICC is composed of representatives from the Ministry of Foreign Trade and Investment (MINEXC), the Ministry of Finance and Planning (MINEF), the Ministry of Agriculture (MINAG), the National Institute of Hydraulic Resources (INIHAR). This ICC will provide oversight to the National Project Steering Committee (NPSC) which is responsible for overseeing the implementation of GCF projects in Cuba. This information will be reflected in the project document that will be signed between UNDP and AMA.

The NPSC shall consist at least once a year or more as necessary:

- An Executive that holds the project ownership and chairs the NPSC.
- A Development Partner representing the interests of the parties concerned that provide funding and/or technical expertise to the project. This role will be filled by CTIMA and UNDP.
- Beneficiaries/Comprises of Ministry of Agriculture (MINAG), National Institute of Hydraulic Resources (INIHAR) in collaboration with other entities such as: Ciego de Avila, Camagüey, Las Tunas, Granma, Artemisa, Mayabeque, Ciego de Avila, Camagüey, Las Tunas and Granma.

Project Assurance is a critical component of the project assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional and headquarters levels. The quality assurance role supports the Project Manager to ensure that the project is being implemented according to the approved project document and that appropriate project management milestones are managed and completed. Project Assurance must be independent of the Project Management Team and should not report directly to the Project Manager. The project assurance role is covered by the accredited entity level provided by the GCF.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 29 OF 67

B

As an Implementing Entity to the GCF, UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance services including: (i) Day-to-day oversight supervision; (ii) Oversight of project completion; (iii) Oversight of project reporting.

## National Project Director
The National Project Director has the authority to execute the project daily activities on behalf of UNDP. The National Project Director will be responsible for ensuring that the project generates and manages making project decisions daily. His/her main responsibility is to ensure that the project generates the maximum impact in terms of outputs, outcomes, and results while keeping within budgetary and cost limitations. The Implementing Partner (IAMA) appoints the National Project Director, who must be a different person from the IAMA’s Country Director. The National Project Director will be responsible for oversight of two separate Coordinations (Technical Coordination and an Operational Coordination) each headed by a Coordination Team Leader. The Technical Coordination Team will be comprised of local staff that will be contracted through UNDP financing. The Operational Coordination Team will be comprised of local staff that will be contracted by UNDP through IAMA financing.

## The Technical Coordination
The Technical Coordination will be headed by a Technical Coordinator for the support of the project’s technical coordination and integration with other projects in the country. The Technical Coordination will assist in: 1) the economic sectors and local government to integrate EBA strategies and find financial mechanisms (i) Planning Specialized Teams (PSTs) that will work with local governments to integrate EBA strategies into sectoral processes; ii) Legal Management Specialist that will work with local and national governments to integrate EBA mechanisms into existing legal frameworks. The Technical Coordination Team Leader will be responsible for the technical coordination in establishing and integrating long term OM strategies for the project outputs (as defined by the National Project Director). The Technical Coordination Team Leader will report directly to the technical coordination in establishing and integrating long term OM strategies for the project outputs as defined by the National Project Director. The Technical Coordination Team Leader will be responsible for the operational Coordination and will be in charge of the project’s administrative and financial management.

## The Operational Coordination
The Operational Coordination will be headed by an Operational Coordinator for the support of the project’s operational coordination and integration with other projects at provincial level. The Operational Coordination Teams comprised of provincial and municipal representatives from GTMA, MINAG and INRH at each provincial/municipal level. The Operational Coordination Team Leader will be responsible for the operational coordination in establishing and integrating long term OM strategies for the project outputs (as defined by the National Project Director). The Operational Coordination Team Leader will report directly to the technical coordination in establishing and integrating long term OM strategies for the project outputs as defined by the National Project Director.

## Flow of Funds
As the AE UNDP Cuba will disburses funds (received from the GCF according to the FAA development schedule) to the implementing partner as per the requests received from AE at the EE level on the project workplan. Also, AEPA will conclude agreements with its absorbed institutions and centers, and with entities belonging to MINAG and INRH in order to implement the project activities. The AE UNDP Cuba will be responsible for direct disbursement of funds to the implementing partner and to the absorbers as per the requests received from the expected results. Considering the restrictions of the blockade that limit the capacity of Cuba’s importing institutions, the AE UNDP Cuba will ensure that all necessary arrangements are made in advance so that this project the implementing partner coincides with the responsible party since AEPA through its National Project Director.

### B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

196. Rising sea levels and increased storms will have a considerable impact on coastal communities through coastal erosion, flooding, and saltwater intrusion. These impacts are expected to be more severe in the eastern part of Cuba's most vulnerable population.

197. By the year 2050, it is estimated that 2,465 km² (CC = normal conditions) will be affected by sea-level rise, with an additional 388 km² under SLR and 2100 mSLR. This conversion is made up of 74% coastal erosion, 20% flooding, and 6% saltwater intrusion.

198. The GCF has provided critical skills to implement the climate adaptation strategy through "Tarea Vida." This has helped to reduce vulnerability and poverty; however, additional support is required to address key barriers to facilitate its implementation in a more integrated manner. These barriers include limited access to technical and financial resources, as well as limited institutional capacity.

199. The GCF's focus on climate change adaptation and disaster risk management is aligned with the Cuban government's priorities for sustainable development. However, the GCF's funding is not sufficient to address all of Cuba's needs in this area. As a result, the Cuban government has been forced to seek additional financing from other sources.

of access to international capital markets. Balancing the budget from further cuts in expenditure would impair GCF’s ability to provide essential services such as health and education.

GCF will support the development of a national coastal resilience strategy, which will be implemented as follows: The project will develop key instruments and information products, as well as ensure EBA inclusion on policy and regulation frameworks for ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) in Cuba. These include developing a national coastal resilience strategy, which will serve as a blueprint for the country’s response to climate change. The key instruments will support the national uptake of ecosystem-based terrestrial planning and will provide the national capacity to implement EBA at scale through the development of a national coastal resilience strategy and associated alternatives. This includes information creation related not only with the implementation of ecosystem-based management (EBM) but also with the identification of alternative livelihoods, including those that are climate-resilient.

The project will support the integration of climate change adaptation into the country’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP), as well as the country’s adaptive capacity to climate realities and sector actions according on short, medium and long term goals. This strategy will be developed in close collaboration with the Cuban government and other stakeholders, including civil society.

The project will also support the development of a national coastal resilience strategy that will address the challenges that might appear, in fact a consistent key to address the uncertainty related with climate change. GCF resources will be used to develop a national coastal resilience strategy that will provide a framework for addressing climate risks as one that looks to empower coastal communities to the ongoing impact produced by climate change and sea-level rise.

### 205. The project has been designed to facilitate the implementation of "Tara Vida" which will mobilize all sectors and stakeholders in Cuba for the conservation, restoration and sustainable use of mangroves and seagrasses. Tara Vida is a multi-sectoral initiative that aims at increasing awareness on the importance of mangroves and seagrasses as natural coastal defences and their role in providing ecosystem’s functionalities restoration, awareness creation and evidence-based EBA. Implementing strategies for the conservation, restoration and sustainable use of mangroves and seagrasses will provide a strong technical and institutional level for the correct implementation of Tara Vida (see Table 2), including updating and strengthening the national coastal resilience strategy, developing an information campaign on ecosystem-based management and information products ensuring communication flow (both down, bottom up and transversal) and the revised national coastal resilience strategy. The project will also support the development of a national coastal resilience strategy that will address the challenges that might appear in fact a consistent key to address the uncertainty related with climate change.

207. Furthermore, results from the project will enable municipal governments to take full advantage of the secondary benefits resulting as a result of the current constitutional reform process. The information products foreseen by the project will be used in the development of new territorial and sectoral plans, as well as in the preparation of national and territorial planning, as well as their direct implementation. As these constitutional changes are recent, effective implementation is required to enable effective climate related risks reduction and integration into policies and development actions. GCF, EEA and other financial mechanisms will be used for this purpose. The project will also support the creation of mobilization structures such as CEA’s and CBRS for EEA that allow for enhanced local capacities to better address climate change adaptation needs.

208. Finally, the information collected through this project represents an important opportunity for alternative solutions for addressing climate change impacts in South America. It is expected that the results will be used by other countries within the EEA approach and is more accessible and in tune with developing small island nations and their national contexts.


## B.2 Climate Change Adaptation

209. The project has been designed in close consultation and investment of relevant municipal governments at all the stages of its implementation, including the identification of the interventions, ongoing capacity building programs and information management approaches, all of which provide a sustainable framework for the investments. By ensuring that the investments are sustained through national frameworks, the project ensures that the investments as well as the results of the interventions are sustained beyond the project period.

210. The project is aligned with the constitutional reform process in Argentina, specifically with the law “Tarea Vida” and with the constitutional reform process that has taken place and elevates the role of municipal governments.


## Operation & Maintenance (OM) arrangements

211. Operation & Maintenance (OM) arrangements are in place with national and local governments and private sector entities for the maintenance of the acquired equipment, as well as for the replacement of spare parts on a long-term basis detailed in the project’s OM Plan (Annex 13). These include the commitment of CUP 306.78 million* by the GCF to cover the cost of OM and maintenance services for the acquired equipment over its expected lifespan, with specialized national technical institutions such as the Institute of Ceramics, Mathematics and Physics to provide support.

212. Equipment to be acquired through the project has been selected with the support of national experts identifying priority needs in terms of climate change adaptation and ensuring that the equipment is designed for long-term use with free spare parts and considered within the project budget. The purchase of the required replacement parts for the equipment will be covered by the OM Plan, which includes a commitment to cover 10% of the total cost of the acquired equipment over its expected lifespan (post-50 years). This is in line with the GCF’s policy on long-term maintenance and repair services. The OM Plan also provides for the replacement of normal lifespan as a result of national innovations and careful maintenance plans in specialized centers throughout the country.

213. As established by the GCF, projects implemented derived from international collaboration are implemented by national governments and their partners. In this case, the project is being implemented with the support of national experts. These investments ensure that all equipment designed for its functioning is maintained. Through Activity 2.3, work will be carried out to ensure that the acquired equipment is integrated into the existing infrastructure systems and capacity building systems; these will complement actions and investments foreseen by CTIMA to reduce climate change impacts in South America. The OM Plan also includes a commitment of CUP 10 million for an indicator included within the project.


## Project Implementation and Operation & Maintenance plan

214. A Project Implementation and operation and maintenance plan has been developed as part of project development with maintenance costs being foreseen by national counterparts to ensure that these are included within national budgetary frameworks. The OM Plan also includes a commitment for the replacement parts required for equipment and guaranteeing that in the case of technical equipment service contracts are included to provide OM and CBRS services, as well as the hiring of specialized personnel to carry out maintenance tasks. These will be hired by the project to provide support to national institutions in the design of GCF plans of the project investments.

215. Additionally, a Project Implementation Plan has been developed with limited resources foreseen for ongoing management and monitoring activities. The OM Plan also includes a commitment of CUP 30 million for limitedly required limitations for ongoing management will ensure the project’s Technical sustainability. For example, the OM Plan provides for the hiring of specialized personnel to carry out maintenance tasks in the Adaptation Fund project in Argentina and Mayaguez. The strategies will also be based on, and backed-up by, national frameworks and institutions.


* Valued at 16.5million USD considering US$ 1+24 CUP Exchange Rate.

**1.** The project will ensure that all major stakeholders are involved in the design of the project, with a sound understanding of the main drivers of ecosystems functions' optimization (as natural infrastructure) or degradation and investing on those drivers to enhance ecosystem resilience. A particular focus is given to coastal zones where the interplay between land use change and climate change impacts is particularly important. The integrated ecosystems functionality approach, which applied to coastal zones recognized as a priority area for adaptation in the GCF portfolio, will be used to design an adaptive management system that integrates natural infrastructure with human activities, ensuring the ability of coastal areas to cope and respond to natural dynamics, including climate related events.

This information will inform the mainstreaming of EBA into the local municipality's development plans and updated to reflect new developments.

**8.** Social sustainability will be ensured through the active and direct involvement of the populations affected by, or at risk from climate change impacts in the project design and implementation process as well as in the adaptation strategies, as active participants rather than solely as stakeholders. ### D.1 PROJECT OUTCOMES (approximately 7 pages)

222. Tools and capacity building platforms developed through this project will directly assist the GoC in providing climate-resilient coastal infrastructure, including mangrove restoration and other ecosystem-based adaptation measures. This will enable the implementation of the GoC’s “Taina Vida” while prioritizing the adaption model presented in Section 5.3.

223. The coasts in Cuba will be greatly affected by coastal flooding from R.R and increased storm intensity, as well as ocean acidification and sea level rise. This is expected to have a significant impact on the country’s coastal infrastructure (FS Section 4.6) and over 57% of the Cuban population resides along the coastline, hence the project will directly contribute to reducing climate risks for this large proportion of the Cuban population, but also for its high potential for national uptake.

224. Project outcomes are expected to be achieved by: i) increasing mangrove ecosystems and ecosystem services, specifically those related to coastal protection; and ii) increasing resilience of most vulnerable coastal communities through capacity building and improved access to climate information and regulations. The project has targeted two of the most climatically vulnerable coastal stretches in Cuba as identified by national authorities.

225. Project outputs have been designed to directly contribute in reducing climate risks along highly vulnerable coastal areas, including: i) increasing mangrove cover; ii) improving forest management practices; iii) reducing coastal erosion and provide a buffer to increased storm intensity and changing precipitation patterns. Together with the capacity building activities, these will increase the resilience of coastal communities’ access to tailored climate information for enhanced decision making at municipal scale, and mainstreaming environmental considerations in local planning processes.

226. The project is expected to directly benefit 105,347 inhabitants along a 1,300km coastline are the direct and indirect beneficiaries of this intervention. The economic importance and size of coastal communities in Cuba makes them particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

Furthermore, the current constitutional reforms provide an important opportunity to engage local communities and develop new governance structures that will ensure the implementation of national adaptation frameworks and ensure climate change risks are considered and addressed.

### D.2 PROJECT IMPLEMENTATION

Mangrove restoration and knowledge management from project implementation is a strong asset included within the project, by which it goes to generate lessons learned, including information products, to enhance national and international efforts in mangrove conservation and coastal ecosystem-based adaptation. The project will implement an exclusively hard infrastructure approaches

### D.3 PROJECT IMPACTS

The project will focus on a combination of natural regeneration and artificial regeneration (planting) of mangroves to achieve the rehabilitation of degraded areas and increase forest cover, as well as improving the resilience of ecosystems in mangroves and other associated coastal ecosystem services. Improved management of forest will contribute to emission reductions through increased carbon sequestration and improved soil quality.

Mangroves are unique carbon storehouses in their ability to lock carbon up in anaerobic soils. Globally, blue carbon ecosystems (mangroves, seagrass and salt marshes) store more than 10 times the amount of carbon per year for mangroves. For a total extent of 112,477 ha, the total annual sequestration is 98,844 t CO₂/year, thus the expected impact on climate change mitigation will be significant.

Seagrass and salt marshes are also important carbon sinks and provide other ecosystem services such as grazing grasslands will also have an important sequestration effect. However, as national formulas are still being developed, a numerical value for this impact is not yet available. Mangroves and seagrasses in Cuba that have cross cutting results, it is classified as a pure adaptation project in accordance to the GoC’s preference.

## Methodology to calculate beneficiaries

230. The number of direct and indirect beneficiaries were established by outputs, considering the expected benefits derived from the project activities and the capacity building actions that will be implemented in each settlement for rehabilitation in specific coastal sites. In this case beneficiaries were estimated as the number of people who will benefit directly or indirectly from the project activities and capacity building actions to enhance communities' resilience to climate change and extreme events have been considered in this estimation.

231. The direct beneficiaries (DB) are defined as those people living in the settlements affected by climate change, including coastal erosion, saltwater intrusion, and flooding. These settlements affected by climate 5 humanities, and saline intrusion, located in areas within the influence of mangrove forests and coral reefs, and will be directly targeted by SLN beneficiaries (DB) according to SLC criteria up to 80 m for each of the municipalities of the target areas (24 municipalities).

### Output 1

232. Direct Beneficiaries (DB) were established in the settlements where direct benefits will be received as the result of project activities and/or capacity building actions that are expected to be directly affected by SLN beneficiaries (DB) and humanities.

Output 2

234. Indirect Beneficiaries (IB) are defined as those people living in other municipalities who will benefit from the capacity building in this component. Hence, Direct Beneficiaries (DB) are calculated based on the number of settlements that will be directly targeted by SLN beneficiaries within the municipalities as a whole; 60% of its population will be targeted for training.

235. Indirect Beneficiaries (IB) are defined as those people living in other municipalities who will benefit from the results of the capacity building but will not receive direct training.

#### Potential benefits

236. The project aims to contribute to the development and implementation of a climate resilient in coastal areas in Cuba resulting in the implementation of a full-scale, integrated and sustained application of EBA while integrating local communities' knowledge and practices into the design and implementation of EBA.

237. While Output 1 will enhance coastal ecosystem services to better manage climate impacts facing vulnerable coastal communities, Output 2 will contribute to the development of an enabling environment for EBA by involving passive stakeholders into a preventive/proactive approach where they are informed active agents in natural resource management and conservation. This will be achieved through capacity building actions that will be integrated within local planning frameworks for long-term LBA sustainability. Through this output, the project provides an important contribution to the development of a climate resilient coastal zone in Cuba by strengthening local coastal planning mechanisms, moving beyond the implementation of top-down directives; furthermore current EBA and other related policies will be strengthened through the integration of local knowledge and experience that will be scaled-up within the framework of the long-term program "Tierra de Vida".

238. The EBA approach approved by the project allows for a cost-effective strategy to coastal adaptation that builds on existing capacities, resources, and institutions in the coastal zone. 239. The project will generate valuable lessons and best practices, which will be systematized and shared with other stakeholders through a series of workshops and training sessions. 293. The marine biodiversity in Cuba is one of the most important in the Caribbean region, being a breeding site of keystone and vulnerable species. Marine biodiversity in Cuba is among the highest in the Caribbean, and coastal wetlands provide critical habitat for many of these species. These wetlands are also essential to mitigate future loss of native flora and fauna, and also support the continued functioning of both local and migratory species. This action will contribute to the conservation of marine biodiversity by reducing the impacts of climate change on the coast, strengthening biodiversity corridors, improving air quality and enabling blue carbon sequestration - a critical part of the global effort to mitigate climate change. Coastal wetlands are also important for fisheries (including seagrasses) have the capacity to permanently store carbon, unhealthy and weak ones are potentially great sources of greenhouse gas emissions. The action will contribute to the conservation of marine biodiversity by ensuring the maintenance of healthy ecosystems.

294. Building on the work done in previous projects, this project aims to improve an understanding of climate change impacts and solutions, including the integration of ecosystem management in coastal planning and disasters risk reduction strategies. This will be achieved through a participatory approach that involves local communities as well as public sector and research organizations, it will be ensured that activities are tailored to the local ecosystems and needs of the communities involved. The project will also ensure that the project is aligned with priorities of stakeholders. Moreover, community awareness and sensitization campaigns that promote ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) in coastal wetlands will be conducted. These actions will contribute to key supporting local, sustainable management of coastal ecosystems. Intersectoral coordination and continuous environmental education are essential for ensuring that the impacts on the ecosystem are managed through improved coastal planning and adjusted economic planning instruments.

Access to clean water, both in terms of quantity and quality, is the greatest challenge in which women are more vulnerable than men. Women also have limited access to land and natural resources.

A Gender Action Plan (Action X) has been designed by considering both a comprehensive gender assessment, as well as the specific needs of women and girls living in coastal wetlands. The action plan will support wetland water filtering capacity and water quality through waste management and sanitation activities (GGC Foundation). This project will also contribute to the implementation of the Action Plan, which includes gender-specific co-benefits. Moreover, training and capacity-building will further support Cuba in its continued promotion of women's participation in coastal wetlands management and conservation efforts as well as opportunities to benefit from project activities. The increased participation of women in the forest sector within the country is also expected to contribute to the implementation of the Action Plan.

## Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population

### Cuba: A small island developing state (SIDS) with a high vulnerability to climate change, in fact it is central to the Nation's vision of its future. The National Environmental Strategy of Cuba (NES) and "Tarea Vida" have been developed as part of the Cuban government's response to the challenges posed by climate change.

### Cuba's economy has traditionally been based on agriculture, fishing, tourism, and manufacturing. However, the country is currently facing a number of economic challenges that are likely to be exacerbated by climate change. These include:

- **Agriculture:** Climate change is expected to have significant impacts on crop yields and water availability.
- **Fishing:** Changes in sea temperature and salinity levels could affect fish populations and fishing grounds.
- **Tourism:** Rising sea levels, increased frequency of extreme weather events (e.g., hurricanes), coastal flooding due to sea penetration and extreme weather events, coral destruction, and mangrove damage are all potential threats to the tourism industry.

### The Cuban government has recognized these challenges and is taking steps to address them. For example:

- **NES:** This strategy aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 20% by 2030.
- **Tarea Vida:** This program focuses on sustainable development, including climate change adaptation and socio-economic development.

### Cuba's experience in adapting to climate change has been limited due to the lack of resources required. The project will provide GCF and GGC resources to address this barrier with funds being invested in a range of activities that are aligned with the priorities set out by the NES and Tarea Vida, as well as the GGC and that are required to correctly and sustainably implement EBA as a main strategy for adaptation.

### The project will also provide technical assistance to build capacity at all levels (national, provincial, municipal) across diverse stakeholders and institutional platforms to plan for and manage coastal ecosystem rehabilitation, and to

**promote climate-resilient development and enhance household resilience through capacity building, regulatory framework modification and community resilient interventions. ### 2.0 Co-financing

**Co-financing through cross-sectoral integration facilitated through the KMPCA and constant monitoring, the project ensures that planning and future coastal interventions recognize the interrelation of climate change impacts with other development priorities such as disaster risk reduction (DRR), food security, health, etc., in a holistic, preventive approach based on an understanding of the strong dependence of the natural infrastructure of Caribbean countries on the environment and its resources.**

**This project will provide co-financing for the implementation of the State Plan to Combat Climate Change (Tara de Vida) which will benefit all small island, local and national Caribbean economies.**

### 2.1 Access to Co-Financing

**International socio-political circumstances have resulted with the GCF having to forego limited access to co-financing for projects in the Caribbean region. This has led to a lack of capacity building and implementation of environmental and natural resource management policies and projects. This is mainly due to lack of access to hard currency, which is required to implement these projects. The project will provide co-financing through technical solutions required to address climate change impacts.**

**The GCF has identified the need for co-financing in this project. While GCF co-financing has been dedicated to enhancing project results, this co-financing is possible by the Government Directives which will provide the necessary resources and support to implement the State Plan to Combat Climate Change needs in support of the implementation of the State Plan to Combat Climate Change (Tara de Vida).**

### 2.2 New Co-Financing Mechanism

**Cuba has a complex institutional system. There are many institutions, all of which directly or indirectly, achieve to mobilize resources for climate change adaptation and mitigation projects. The project will benefit from this solid institutional network; there is a tendency to work in silos looking to meet directives that follow a step-down approach. This results in the lack of coordination between different actors, leading to inefficiencies, greater efficiency and productivity. Lack of communication equipment and infrastructure capacity that constitutes communication networks and information sharing systems are also major challenges for Cuba despite the existing capacity and will remain as researches.**

**All these factors highlight the need to identify gaps and opportunities, in that a comprehensive solution requires integrated actions and local specific solutions. Hence the project looks to invest in these capacities at a national level, which will be used by the different actors involved in the project. The project will also generate specific tools to strengthen resilience. 66% of the total project budget will flow to local communities, favoring their capacity building and strengthening their role as key stakeholders in climate change adaptation. This will enhance data and information sharing transversally between institutions, which in turn will favor the integration of existing capacities at a national level.**

AMERICA is in charge promoting government strategies for environmental management through research and the active implementation of strategic projects and in putting into practice environmental policy and providing key information to decision-makers. It has expertise in implementing the baseline projects in social, economic, and environmental terms. AMERICA is also in charge of promoting the implementation and execution of international projects, including an extensive portfolio of GEF projects, totaling nearly USD50 million.

284. AMERICA will be responsible for coordinating with other UNDP offices and regional and country representations. Among these is the Institute of Marine Sciences (ICOMAR) with a key role in monitoring project performance and providing technical support to the project team. The project team includes an environmental scientist at the national level as well as a municipal environment specialist at a municipal level. This makes AMERICA a strategic actor in bringing together the necessary expertise for the implementation of the project, which is expected to be implemented as required through Output 2.0. AMERICA will invest in the integration of a specific project management team at a national and municipal level with the aim of ensuring that the project is carried out effectively.

The GCF is aligned with UNDP's comparative advantage in the areas of capacity building, providing technical and policy support, reducing barriers and creating enabling conditions for adaptation planning and implementation, and promoting the integration of climate change into development planning processes. The project will be implemented through a participatory approach that includes all stakeholders as detailed in Table 1 and the project's Feasibility Study. The technical and policy support provided by UNDP through AMERICA will ensure that the project is carried out effectively, efficiently, and with high quality, while being sustainable over time.

286. A wide range of stakeholders were involved in the development of the project proposal. These included government actors involved in climate change and disaster risk management, different population groups from various communities, civil society organizations (CSOs), and representatives of indigenous peoples. They represent the interests of citizens in the Municipal Assembly of Peoples' Power, as well as those in the health and education sectors. The stakeholders were consulted through a participatory approach that included all aspects of social and physical planning. At the national level, the Economy and Planning, Finance and Banking sectors were also consulted to ensure that the project is aligned with the country's development priorities. AMERICA will work closely with the Climate Change Committee (CCC) of the Green Climate Fund.

287. The stakeholders' views and interests were considered in three main areas: aimed 1) to understand and identify their perceptions of climate change and the possible social and environmental risks/impacts on their communities, 2) integrate their views and interests into the project's design and implementation, and 3) ensure that they are informed about the progress of the project, and it indeed identifies other barriers and opportunities. These processes were informed by a gender approach, ensuring that women and men have equal access to information and decision-making in all aspects of the project, and women and men be included. Overall, these activities were successful and had a high level of active participation, allowing for the identification of key issues and concerns related to the project.

288. The active consultation process was a unique experience for the GCF and included participatory mapping, site visits, focus group discussions, and individual interviews with stakeholders. These methods allowed AMERICA to validate the approach and territorial ownership. Additionally, surveys were administered to two communities in a pilot phase of the project. This ensured that all relevant stakeholders had an opportunity to express their views regarding stakeholder views and interests concerning the Project. The tools and information derived from the five stages of consultation will be used as inputs for the stakeholder consideration plan for the implementation of the Project (Annex V).

289. The GCF is seeking a grant to undertake the proposed adaptation actions. For the project, the GCF has set aside $10 million in its national budget. AMERICA will provide technical and policy support to the project team for 30 years with GCF funds being specifically targeted to address required investments needed to implement the project.

289. A GCF grant is deemed at the most appropriate level of concessionality in light of: (i) the public good nature of the proposed adaptation actions; (ii) the need to provide financial support for the implementation of the project and procurement of goods and services required for the implementation of the project's activities and inability to access funding from other sources; (iii) the high level of concessionality in the GCF national budget, taking into account macroeconomic and fiscal circumstances currently affecting Cuba as a result of the trade blockade; and (iv) GCF's significant experience in providing concessional financing for adaptation projects. AMERICA will provide the necessary adaptation investments to implement an integral adaptation approach in Cuba has in fact been identified


---
**Page 49**
---

291. The economic analysis developed through the project proposal estimated the net present value (NPV) of the proposed intervention to be $72 million with an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 23.8%, hence demonstrating its financial viability. This is consistent with the findings in Table 3, which shows a 10% discount rate increase as the analysis solely took into consideration investment in mangroves and not the project's innovative function to provide ecosystem services that are also provided by other coastal ecosystems such as coral reefs and seagrass beds.

292. To gauge the impact of the interventions, the project relied initially on a study from the University of Camarioca that assessed the quantity of land flooded, the number of people flooded, and the total stock of assets lost for extreme events in Cuba. The study also estimated the economic value of mangroves by using the replacement cost method. This assessment of mangroves provides an estimate of the benefits of mangroves. Given a total of 409,000 hectares (ha) of mangroves included in the study, this represents approximately 15% of all mangrove habitat in Cuba.

293. This study was complemented with an economic analysis adjusted for the project for which we examine solely the benefits of mangroves in terms of losses of assets along the coast lines of the targeted areas. This assumption underpins the calculation of the net present value (NPV) and internal rate of return (EIRR). The project intervention is expected to provide benefits to communities beyond immediate protection services as well as the long-term benefits derived to the project beneficiaries that are not captured in this analysis, such as improved livelihoods for coastal communities through increased income from enhanced governance mechanisms.

294. Table 3 presents a comparison between two scenarios: one with mangroves and another without them. The table shows the economic damages expected over the next ten years if no action is taken (in US dollars) in the absence of mangroves, as well as the estimated USD 50 million savings that would be achieved within this timeframe by implementing the project intervention.

295. A financial analysis is not applicable to this project as all the impacts to be addressed are exclusively public in nature and will be financed through government resources. The costs associated with mangrove restoration are borne primarily independent private workers who contribute part of their income to the state through tax payments.

296. The economic analysis also includes an estimate of the cost savings that would result from the project intervention for seawalls for coastal protection totaling an estimated US$148 million, or just over 10% of the $5 billion in total costs associated with the construction and maintenance of seawalls along Cuba’s coastlines (see Table 2). The analysis is based on the findings presented in the Feasibility Study Section 5.6. Cost comparisons for the engineering solution took into account costs based on feasibility study assumptions, including a range of costs for seawall construction and maintenance. The analysis calculated coastal protection costs of 19 major cities in the Caribbean and included a range of costs for seawalls with different heights and widths. The cost estimates were based on the average cost per linear meter of seawalls built in the targeted areas would stand between USD 243 million and USD 851 million. Operation and maintenance costs are estimated at $60,000 to $97,000 per year for each kilometer of seawall.

297. The base case economic model, based on the conservative assumptions above, results in an NPV of -$38 million. This is a negative value because it assumes that the project intervention will cost more than its benefits. At a 10% discount rate this is USD -68.8 million. The sensitivity analysis shows that a 20% increase in costs (for instance due to higher construction costs) would result in an NPV of -$93.4 million, while a 50% decrease in benefits (due to lower mangrove restoration costs) would be the decisive factor for this value of project.

externalities (e.g., ecosystem) would push the EBIW to 0.7%. Any further increase in costs and decrease in benefits would push the EBIW clearly into negative territory. The NPV is negative in all scenarios. The comparison provides a clear indication that the project should not be pursued, especially considering the reduced maintenance cost of EBA interventions which require capacity easily found within Cuba with limited resources.

298. Interventions proposed through this project hence favor cost-effective interventions, which while may require high initial investment, are expected to have long-term benefits and provide a resilient solution for coastal communities. System knowledge is the base to provide a resilient solution to the CC derived coastal threats. The project will leverage on the experience gained from the development of EBA projects in Cuba, not international projects that have been developed in Cuba over more than 30 years. This experience will feed into this project and will be used as a basis for the actions of the project. The “Macroproject on Coastal Hazards and Vulnerability” (MCHV) is a project funded by the GCF which has been developed with particular importance as the base for the actions of this project. The MCHV has identified the specific relevance to the challenges of adaptation in coastal zones (See Fig Section 2), namely mangroves, geomorphology and sediment transport, sea level rise, storm surge, wave action, extreme weather events, land use change, intrusion, bathymetric services, ocean acidification and information integration. The best practices developed and lessons learned from the MCHV will be used as a basis for the project. These lessons have been applied to the proposed project approach.

301. The GCF through its previous support has invested and developed in best practices and actions both on the ground and to strengthen the legislation related to the sustainable management of marine-coastal ecosystems. This experience is very important, as it will provide a basis for the development of actions that are relevant to the project objectives. The MCHV has identified and measured various monitoring tools and systems that have been applied in Cuba. These lessons learned from the MCHV will be used as a basis for the project approach to establish the basis and provide valuable inputs for the proposed project (see Table 1).

The project re-established the best bed of red mangrove (Rhizophora mangle) in two geographic sectors (Santiago de Cuba and Cienfuegos). The project also established the best beds of black mangrove (Avicennia germinans), the restoration of the mangroves and swamps forests, the identification of 20 invasive exotic species per site, with proposals for control and management. The project will establish a monitoring system to assess the success of the project (e.g., swamps grasslands, swamp forest); realization of space-temporal analyses of the types of coverage (scourland, coastal dunes, mangroves, swamps forests, etc.) and their evolution; the establishment of an index of vegetation indices and the evaluation of the functioning of freshwater currents. This experience will be scaled up directly through Output 1.

## Activity 1.2
### Management and rehabilitation through restoration of mangroves for coastal protection
#### Inputs:
- Labor and expertise of all local forestry enterprises to implement restoration measures
- Supervision and monitoring work of forest enterprises and local environmental supervision and pest control services
- Restoration equipment provided per target site (e.g., shovels, rakes, etc.)
- Restoration works including protective measures of existing mangroves and planting of new mangroves
- All forestry enterprises and local environmental supervision and pest control services to process restoration monitoring information

### Activity 1.3. Record and assess coastal ecosystem resilience and regeneration based on conditions post restored coastal wetlands
#### Inputs:
- Labor and expertise of all local forestry enterprises to implement restoration measures
- Supervision and monitoring work of forest enterprises and local environmental supervision and pest control services

## Activity 1.4: Enhance water management using large-scale freshwater drainage systems in mangroves and coastal wetlands and enhance its use in target sites

### 1.4.1. Enhance local water management in target sites for selected areas affected by long-term droughts
- **1.4.2**. Invest in monitoring, modeling, and simulations to evaluate the evolution of salt intrusion over time and assess the impacts of SLR and other climate change patterns on coastal conditions.

### Salt intrusion management
- Model salt intrusion that will result in the failure of water supply systems.
- Develop models and tools for simulating and monitoring the evolution of salt intrusion in time, based on the water flows and salt concentration (using long-term periods).
- Modeling of liquid and solid salt intrusion evolution in time. The model is applied to a site in 15,039 ha during the project's initial 2 years.

### Inputs
- Real-time hydrologic and hydraulic simulations.
- Data for decision-making on managing saline intrusion. Water quality data will be collected at key locations distributed along the course of the main drainage system and at critical water levels distributed within the area affected by the salt wedge advance. The real-time hydrostatic points to be located at the mouths of major rivers and in areas upstream and downstream.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Risk factors and mitigation measures (see pages [3-9])

For probability: High has high applicability; Medium has moderate applicability; Low has negligible probability. For impact: High has significant impact, Medium has medium impact, and Low has low or no impact. Mitigation measures include the removal of errors, corruption,enstein agency weaknesses, as well as fraudulent, coercive, collusive, and obstructive practices.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

### Category
- Technical and operational

### Probability
- Low

### Impact
- Medium

### Description
Limited engagement of local actors in the implementation of the project activities which leads to a lack of appropriation of the additional initiatives affecting their sustainability in the long term. (Mitigation Measures)

The project has been designed with very close collaboration with communities and local actors to address their vulnerability and needs, as well as in alignment with an existing comprehensive national strategy for Climate Change Adaptation, which has strong institutional support from the government. The Social and Environmental Performance Framework (SEP) has been developed along with consultations that are included in Annex XIX. Consultations have been included in the project design phase to ensure that the project is aligned with the needs of communities and will contribute to building capacity on adaptation and the benefits of EBA, safeguards and climate change in general. The Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) has been developed based on lessons learned from previous projects and includes a set of methodologies tailored to the actors where consultations programs were implemented. Best practices for community engagement have been identified by the project team and will be shared with other stakeholders. The project will engage communities in a manner that is relevant existing institutions and mechanisms such as Capacity Building Centers and Multi-stakeholder Forums (MSF). The project will also ensure that the project steering committee, which includes a member of the Project Steering Committee (see also C.7) what will increase their influence on the project and their ability to provide feedback in a timely manner. The project team has considered the need for an engagement plan as part of the product of high value to the communities. The engagement will also be part of the evaluation of the project. Actions will be taken to ensure that this happens.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

### Category
- Technical and operational

### Probability
- Medium

### Impact
- Medium

### Description
Extreme weather events could affect the restoration activities during the first half of project implementation. The outputs of the project, particularly output 1, might be affected by extreme weather events which could cause damages, loss or delay in the achievement of the outputs. This risk is considered as a low probability because the actions taken within the first half of the project (actions taken during the former 4 years) will have increased ecosystem resilience to these events. This uncertainty is partially mitigated through the use of resilient species and techniques that are more resistant to climate change, but this does not completely eliminate the risk.

Restoration interventions will be implemented based on site-specific protocols derived from evidence-based practices developed by the project team in collaboration with local actors. The results of the restoration activities will be monitored and will be published available in 2023[19], consider local environmental conditions—including frequency, severity and type of extreme weather events—will be considered to determine which species are most appropriate for each site. The types of historical climate-induced events will be used to detail behind the types of interventions at the local level. Extreme events such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and cold spells can have a significant impact on restoration conditions, could delay restoration processes. Restoration methods that consider small plants vulnerable to certain environmental conditions (e.g., drought) may not be suitable for these conditions. The project team will use techniques such as (reducing the impact): i) planting from nurseries (when mangrove trees have a certain height and thus reducing as much as possible the risk of death due to extreme weather events); ii) using species that are more resistant to climate change; iii) evaluating survival rates and growth of ecosystem restoration through a comprehensive monitoring program, which will be shared with other stakeholders.

[19] Manglar Vivo (2020). Protocolos de restauración de manglares.  
Manglar Vivo (2020). Preguntas de ecotecnología para la restauración ecológica

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

Reduced effectiveness because activities are not undertaken to tackle the causes driving ecosystem degradation.

### Mitigation Measures (GCF)

The project will develop actions to restore ecosystems, including their coastal protective role, while using national funds and initiatives to address the causes of degradation by implementing measures to tackle the cause of species invasions. The GCF will support the implementation of a National Ecosystems Management Strategy (NEMS) Study), including elaborating and implementing regulations specific to each entity. Supporting the capacity building at the regional level, in particular for the development of national strategies and plans, is also included as an action to reduce pressure on ecosystems which are already being carried out and budgeted for. Furthermore, output 2 addressing training of technical staff will be implemented through the GCF.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

### Description

Rehabilitation of ecosystems may introduce non-native organisms/invasive species. The project will rely on a combination of natural regeneration and artificial regeneration (planting) to achieve the recovery of structure, function and EBA services in degraded coastal wetlands. Natural regeneration is preferred because it is more cost-effective than planting (and already exists in the ecosystem) and proliferate and colonize areas more quickly than native species, especially when there are gaps in the vegetation cover.

### Mitigation Measures

Natural regeneration will be the preferred option for rehabilitation of ecosystems. Where enrichment planting and vegetation restoration is carried out, only native species that are adapted to the hydrological and soil conditions of these systems will be used. Species selection will be based on their ability to resist extreme climate-related events (e.g., droughts, floods), to resist invasive species), resist the forces of extreme climate related events, and restore connections and functionalities between ecosystems. The project will also include a monitoring program for the recovery of degraded coastal wetlands and restoration soils, all of which occurring naturally in similar environments in Cuba. The monitoring program will be complemented by an action plan that includes the use of biological control agents (e.g., Trichogramma pretiosa) to combat Leucania inscripta). The selected control methods (primarily manual) will be designed to mitigate adverse effects on already degraded natural habitats and related risks, such as sediment movement, water contamination and equipment and material settlement from any possible exposure. For manual or mechanical control, measures will be taken to avoid damage to the environment and minimize the risk of spreading invasive species. Based on experience from previous project, the project ensures to create the adequate protocols and actions to reduce further the chances of introducing new invasive species.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

### Description

Project activities, including restoring hydrological flow in rivers and coastal wetlands may cause adverse impacts to already degraded natural habitats. Risks related to sediment movement, water contamination and equipment and

Protocols and management measures will be used to ensure workers safety and mitigate damages and disruptions due to the project activities. Health and safety training including first aid and emergency response procedures will be provided to all workers involved in activities which may expose them to vector borne disease exposure such as protective appropriate clothing. Likewise, only certified divers with ample experience shall be allowed to perform any diving activity.

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

325. A gender assessment was carried out in the design of the project, as an entry point for the incorporation of gender perspectives into the project’s activities and outputs. The gender assessment is included in the draft of the gender action plan. The gender action plan for the project was designed by taking into account both a comprehensive gender analysis and the results of the community consultations. The gender action plan addresses the following issues: gender equality, covered women issues, inquired of both institutional and community participants what specific problems and difficulties they face as women, and how these can be addressed through the project’s activities. A summary of the results of the community level consultations can be found in the Stakeholder Consultation and Engagement Plan (SCEP) document, which is available on the GCF website.

326. Cuba has a Gender Inequality Index (GII) of 0.304 (below the average of LAC), which has positioned it as “Group 1” out of five groups. The GII is defined by the UNDP as “countries with high inequality in HDI achievements between women and men, where women have a very low level of education and are underrepresented in the labour force, and opportunities between men and women pace.” Cuba’s overall score at 0.745, ranking it 25th in the world.

338. The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by UNDP financial rules and regulations as well as the United Nations Common Procurement Guidelines (CPG) and the UNDP Procurement Rules and Operations Policies and Procedures (POPPI). These are available here. UNDP has comprehensive procurement policies in place to ensure that all projects comply with these guidelines.

339. The project will be implemented following the National Implementation Modality (NIM) following the UNDP POPPI guidelines, which includes a detailed description of the financial management and procurement procedures as well as an evaluation of capacity. Following the Framework for Cash Transfers to Implementing Partners (part of the Harmonized Guidelines for Cash Transfer), the project will be implemented using cash transfers.

340. The project will be implemented in accordance with the UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations, which are followed by all UNDP projects. These rules and regulations are subject to change from time to time as new guidelines are corporately developed and adopted by UNDP and are fully compliant with UNDP’s procurement and financial management policies.

341. The national executing entity AMACITMA (also referred to as the national Implementing Partner in UNDP documents) will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities comply with the UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations, including the CPG guidelines. In legal terms, this is ensured through the national Government’s signature of a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU), which sets out the financial management procedures to be followed by the Implementing Partner given the use of funds. Both of these documents require compliance with the UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations as well as the CPG guidelines. The MoU will also set out its policies and procedures, and any specific requirements in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) and project confirmation to be agreed with the GCF. This may include, but is not limited to monitoring missions, etc.

342. In line with UNDP’s National Implementation Guidelines (NGI), the Government is responsible for the management and procurement of the Project. The NGIs are available here. The NGIs set out the financial management procedures that must be followed in accordance with the Financial Regulations and Rules of UNDP Financial Regulations section 15.05 et seq.

343. Results derived from the HACT assessment concluded a general low risk level of AMA with questions regarding funds flow, project implementation, and compliance with UNDP policies and procedures. The HACT framework consists of four processes: (i) planning; (ii) procurement; (iii) contract management; and (iv) assurance activities.

Assurance activities include planning, periodic on-site reviews (spot checks), programmatic monitoring, scheduled audits and internal control assessments. A spot check is a quick review to determine whether the project is addressing the gaps. When a spot check finds that the gaps are not addressed it will mean that the level of assurance for the AMA will be reduced. The results from the HACT assessment will be used as input in the planning process, and any necessary adjustments will be made to address the issues identified.

341. The UNDP audit policy provides guidance on the scope and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA. # Secretariat’s assessment of FP157

## Proposal name: Coastal Resilience to Climate Change in Cuba through Ecosystem Based Adaptation - "Mi COSTA"

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Country/ies): Republic of Cuba

### Project/programme size: Small

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks.

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The integrated set of EBA interventions is based on scientifically supported best practices. Ecosystem science, however, is still in its infancy and it is difficult to assess the marginal benefit of each intervention. | The proposed restoration activities targeting coastal communities from flooding, salinity intrusion and storms, the present study does not consider the existing grey infrastructure and is the most appropriate measure for enhancing coastal resilience. However, evidence of the additional climate resilience derived from the proposed restoration remains limited as the scope of ecosystem restoration to be undertaken in this project is small. |
| The proposal also highlights the local community capacities of Cuban specialists and local communities’ involvement through monitoring processes that measures the impact of the interventions on coastal and marine ecosystems functions and adaptive management practices according to measured indicators. | Cuba’s limited foreign exchange earnings are one of the principal barriers preventing the Government of Cuba from acquiring the necessary equipment for this project. Private insurance instruments are also not available in Cuba. GCF involvement will make it possible to acquire the necessary equipment and gain access to international expertise. However, the use of private insurance as an appropriate justification for this approach, absent potentially more efficient private sector financing mechanisms with the same objectives, is outside the scope of this technical review. |

In the base case, the net present value of the proposed intervention is estimated to USD 72 million with an internal rate of return of 21%, demonstrating the overall economic efficiency of the proposed investment project.

Changes in monetary policy may affect the cost of goods and services in Côte d'Ivoire, which are difficult to predict. Contingencies have been included for the cost of goods and services procured under the project, including those related to the sale of timber which would only be released if the rise in price for timber exceeds 10% above the fair GCF proceeds exceeds a predetermined threshold.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

### Project background and climate rationale

The proposed project will enhance the adaptive capacity of Côte d'Ivoire's southern coastline to the increased frequency and intensity of hurricanes caused by climate change by holistically rehabilitating coastal land and ecosystems and their interlinked ecosystems and hydrology. This will reduce the vulnerability of mangroves, swamps, and coral reefs to storm surges, flooding and erosion, and channelling freshwater to coastal areas and aquifers. It will also strengthen the capacity of communities in the targeted catchment area to adapt to climate change by enhancing their capacity to utilize and understand the benefits of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA), enhancing information flow between stakeholders and strengthening the regulatory framework for territorial planning.

### Project impact

The project will restore 11,427 ha of mangroves, 1,088 ha of swamp forest and 92 ha of grass woodland in coastal areas, as well as 35 km² of mangrove forests and 134 km² of coral reefs. Together, these ecosystems will provide protection and regulation services along the targeted coastline. Climate change adaptation and EbA training of trainers will target 60% of the population within the targeted catchment area. Capacity building for communities, with the aim of providing training to 60 per cent of the population within the targeted catchment area, will be provided through a community-based approach. Community understanding and participation will strengthen regulatory frameworks and allow for continuous feedback from and to the commission to technical organizations and national and local governments.

### Project financing

The table below provides a breakdown of the project financing streams and uses of funding.


| Table: Project financing |
| --- | --- |
| **Source** | **Amount (USD)** | **Use** | **Amount (USD)** |
| GCF | 23,927,294 | Rehabilitation of coastal ecosystems | 32,562,765 |
| MINAG* | 16,242,680 | Mainstreaming of ecosystem-based adaptation, and capacity-building of communities and institutions | 9,436,227 |
