# Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Rehabilitated coastal ecosystems for enhanced coping capacity to manage climate impacts (Total cost: USD 32.5 million; GCF cost: USD 4.9 million, or 45.7 per cent)

### Objectives:
- To rehabilitate degraded and damaged coastal wetlands along the wider marine coastal landscape to address projected impacts along coastal zones as a consequence of relative sea level rise (coastal flooding and saline intrusion) and extreme weather events.
- The interactions of the four activities in this component will enable the project to effectively implement the following:

#### Activity 1.1: Monitor the baseline functions of coastal wetlands and allow continuous changes in function to be tracked during project implementation
It will re-establish the hydrological connectivity between coastal wetlands and rivers, which will provide to forestry agencies to clean water channels and restore the ecological flow of freshwater. The degraded mangroves, swamps forests and grassland will be removed and managed. These actions will improve the coping capacity of the ecosystems to manage climate change impacts.

#### Activity 1.2: Rehabilitate ecosystem functions in 15,443 ha of degraded coastal wetlands through natural and assisted regeneration of mangroves, swamp forests and grassland at targeted locations
It will re-establish the hydrological connectivity between coastal wetlands and rivers, which will provide to forestry agencies to clean water channels and restore the ecological flow of freshwater. The degraded mangroves, swamps forests and grassland will be removed and managed. These actions will improve the coping capacity of coastal wetlands to flooding (by increasing sediment trapping, increasing soil organic matter content, reducing erosion and improving floodplain function), extreme events (by strengthening the coastal system green structure and reconstructing its protective role with red mangroves) and saltwater intrusion (by restoring water and infiltration capacity).

#### Activity 1.3: Assess and record the restored functional relationships between coastal and marine ecosystems
It will re-establish the hydrological connectivity between coastal wetlands and rivers, which will provide to forestry agencies to clean water channels and restore the ecological flow of freshwater. The degraded mangroves, swamps forests and grassland will be removed and managed. These actions will improve the coping capacity of coastal wetlands to flooding (by increasing sediment trapping, increasing soil organic matter content, reducing erosion and improving floodplain function), extreme events (by strengthening the coastal system green structure and reconstructing its protective role with red mangroves) and saltwater intrusion (by restoring water and infiltration capacity).

#### Activity 1.4: Establish a monitoring programme for the identification of salinity and eutrophication processes that will improve guidance linking local adaptation actions to control saline intrusion
It will re-establish the hydrological connectivity between coastal wetlands and rivers, which will provide to forestry agencies to clean water channels and restore the ecological flow of freshwater. The degraded mangroves, swamps forests and grassland will be removed and managed. These actions will improve the coping capacity of coastal wetlands to flooding (by increasing sediment trapping, increasing soil organic matter content, reducing erosion and improving floodplain function), extreme events (by strengthening the coastal system green structure and reconstructing its protective role with red mangroves) and saltwater intrusion (by restoring water and infiltration capacity).


---
**Page 80**
---

quantity and quality of water drains into coastal ecosystems. This is particularly important because mangroves are key instruments for aquifer recharge during long periods of drought.

### Component 2: Increased technical and institutional capacity for climate change adaptation in coastal communities, governments and economic sectors (Total cost USD 9.4 million; GCF cost USD 7.4 million)

1. This component will focus on working with vulnerable coastal communities and economic sectors to mainstream EBA actions and information into community-based adaptation measures and national policies. It will also support the implementation of the framework of the Taras Vela National plan to manage climate change. It will address key barriers to the successful implementation of EBA, including lack of institutional capacity and currently opportunities for intersectoral coordination to ensure that EBA investments are appropriately by communities and governments in a way that is consistent with national policies and allow for their long-term sustainability.

2. This component will strengthen sectorial and municipal/regional frameworks for the mainstreaming of EBA into coastal ecosystems data from component 1, which will be included in local development, land-use planning, and disaster risk reduction and zoning plans, ensuring a more integrated approach to climate change adaptation. It will also provide opportunities for intersectoral coordination to ensure that EBA investments are appropriately by communities and governments in a way that is consistent with national policies and allow for their long-term sustainability.

3. Activity 2.1 will focus on training communities as active agents by designing appropriate training programs, including the establishment of community-based training centres in each municipality which will act as training centres, and will implement the capacity-building program for coastal communities to mainstream EBA into local development plans.

4. Activity 2.2 will integrate project-derived information, as well from early warning systems (EWS) and national data sets, into the Knowledge Management Platform for Climate Change Adaptation (KMPCA). This will provide a platform that collects, stores, and inform coastal communities on the local capacity of communities and ecosystems to manage climate change impacts. The KMPCA will also be used by the project team to monitor and assess the effectiveness of EBA in reducing vulnerability to climate change. The project will provide contextual information to enhance EWS or climate impacts through an assessment of the current state of knowledge, including the development of a database by incorporating ecosystem capacity to manage climate threats and better assess vulnerability, thus relying on a two-directional flow of information between communities and the KMPCA.

5. This activity will also support the development of a monitoring and evaluation framework for EBA in coastal ecosystems, as well as the development of an integrated monitoring system (IMMS), with the aim of improving disaster risk reduction, natural resources management and EWS and thus increasing the resilience of coastal ecosystems to climate change impacts.

6. In addition, Activity 2.3 will support the mainstreaming of EBA approaches into regulatory and land-land frameworks at the territorial and national levels, as well as the introduction of financial mechanisms for integrating EBA in water resources management plans and strategies for coastal protection. It will address the critical barrier of inter-agency coordination that is typical among GCF projects by providing a framework for the integration of EBA approaches into restoring the functionality of coastal ecosystems for coastal protection, and instructions on how to maintain such functionality will be created as a basis for the integration of EBA approaches into national policies. It will also provide guidance on the role of EBA in water resources management planning. As the project's cost strategy relies on financing from the public sector, it is important that there are clear guidelines and financial mechanisms for integrating financial mechanisms and financial planning strategies, such as forecast-based financing.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The core objective of the proposed project is to protect and restore coastal ecosystems in Cuba, providing critical ecosystem services for vulnerable people on Cuba's hurricane-prone south coast. The project proposes estimated that the project will be able to restore 11,427 ha of seagrass beds and 350 km² of coral reefs. This will provide protection against storm surges, increase the ecosystem health of 928 ha of seagrass beds and 134 km² of coral reef crusts. This will provide protection for 60,000 people (7% of the population) in 25 target municipalities on coastal erosion-prone areas. The project will also benefit 143,114 total beneficiaries (11 per cent of the population) in the 28 target municipalities against coastal erosion, and provide a range of other benefits such as improved water quality and provision of Eba solutions to manage local impacts of climate change. These beneficiaries will be active in the project implementation process through their participation in community-based adaptive management intervention. Economic losses are expected to be reduced by USD 17.8 million by the end of the project cycle (2035), with a further reduction in the long term (over 15 years) following completion and replication.

The significant benefits, coupled with both livelihoods and ecosystems justify the "high" rating for the programme's impact potential. In addition, research suggests that seagrass beds, coral reefs, mangroves, coastal forests, and other coastal ecosystems, seagrass beds and seaweed may have a significant effect in reducing emissions from land use, although this is not yet fully understood.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium-High

The project will enable two key change-tackles way in which coastal resilience is conceived. The first is to change from traditional disaster risk management to an adaptive approach that maximizes the value of ecosystems, in line with national adaptation planning. The second is a change from reactive to proactive approaches to climate change as part of a shift towards a preventive approach where local communities are informed active participants in coastal and disaster risk management.

The project has been designed taking into account the characteristics of Cuba's economy, which places limitations on private sector engagement, and the country's capacity to procure equipment and materials abroad. As a result, the profile of the project has some differences from that of a typical GEF project.

First, a substantial portion of the GEF budget is dedicated towards highly specialized scientific research and development (R&D) activities in order to develop the technology required to acquire these tools, combined with Cuba's significant expertise in operating and maintaining the equipment. This provides confidence that results can be maintained long after project completion.

Secondly, the project will leverage Cuba's strong institutional capacity and the capacity of its public institutions, including local governments and research centres.

Replication of the project will be facilitated by the adoption of a legal framework for Eba that will provide a clear regulatory basis to address some of the key institutional and financial barriers that currently limit the implementation of EBA. The project will generate valuable evidence on the role of ecosystems in reducing coastal vulnerability, which can help better quantify the coping capacity of an integrated landscape of ecosystems, such as (i) the estimation of coral reef and seagrass recovery time once hydrological rehabilitation in the associated catchment has been achieved; (ii) the assessment of how climate change will affect some events and shifting precipitation patterns and their recovery time; (iii) the estimation of


---
**Page 82**
---

morphological effects of the integrated recovered ecosystems to withstand sea level rise, and (iv) the evaluation of their combined effects in reducing the coastal risks of rehabilitating the natural infrastructure.

22 The combination of a solid community-based approach with the involvement of public institutions will be based on learning and evidence-based decision-making, supported by the development of an overall legal framework for EBA, justly the 'medium-high' rating in terms of paradigm shift potential.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The proposed project will promote the conservation and restoration of over 15,000 ha of rehabilitated mangrove coastal ecosystems as well as the preservation of 2,679 km of seagrass beds and 134 km of coral reef crests. In addition to protection against climate change impacts, it is expected that this project will deliver major benefits in terms of the preservation of ecosystems and associated biodiversity, the increase in carbon stocks and water quality from climate change and other impacts and increase groundwater recharge. It will also deliver significant co-benefits for food security, poverty reduction and the removal of 10 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent per year.

The preservation of ecosystem services has wide benefits across the multiple dimensions of sustainable development. The project will deliver positive impacts on fisheries production, increase the availability of non-timber products and result in sustained agricultural productivity. In addition to these direct benefits, the project is expected to contribute to increased coastal recreational spaces, restoration of ecological cultural heritage and greater availability of ecosystem services for human well-being. The potential economic benefit from disaster recovery (estimated at USD 10-278 million per disaster) should be increasingly relevant as climate change increases the frequency and intensity of hurricanes in the Caribbean region. Although these benefits have not been quantified owing to the fact that specific interventions are yet to be defined, there is high confidence that they would be very relevant.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium–High

The programme targets a small island developing State with high vulnerability to climate change and which ranks relatively high on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) index that measures country readiness and vulnerability, with a particularly high score in terms of vulnerability.

The capacity of Cuba to adapt to climate change is mainly limited by two factors: as result of international socioeconomic circumstances, namely lack of finance and lack of access to technology. The Cuban government has been working on the development of EBA approaches for a country with strong skills in the areas of communities and institutions, including spare parts to guarantee the availability of equipment after project completion that can contribute to covering the financing gap.

Cuban institutions, while generally solid in terms of technical capacity and community-level experience, have not yet developed the implementation of EBA approaches. Fundamentally requires the coordination of administration among different sectors. The GCF intervention will provide assistance to bridge this gap.

### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

## Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium-High

* Existing studies agree that EBA interventions are highly co-effective solutions in increasing resilience to climate change while delivering significant social and environmental co-benefits.
* The programme agrees to deliver high value for money, improving the resilience of over 1.3 million people from the present day and over 150,000 ha of highly biodiverse coastal ecosystems through the GCF grant USD 23.4 million.

* The use of grants and funding is considered adequate in this case considering Cuba's unique geographical location and its high vulnerability to climate change. It is expected that Cuba will be able to generate substantial returns and are aimed at increasing the resilience of the most vulnerable communities. Likewise, Cuba's barriers to accessing technology make it necessary to provide a significant amount of funding for capacity building.

* The GCF Secretariat has agreed with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) to ensure that efficiencies are sought where possible through the use of co-financing and other innovative financing mechanisms.

### Cuban institutions, including the Ministry of Agriculture (MINAG), will contribute substantial co-financing, largely in the form of grants, with a value of USD 20.37 million approved by the GCF Board. The inclusion of co-financing in the GCF proposal reflects changes in Cuba's monetary policy have been taken into account in the preparation of the funding proposal and additional resources will be made available to MINAG for this project.

* In order to mitigate the risks associated with the purchasing power of GCF proceeds, the GCF Secretariat has agreed that the Cuban government will provide a 10 per cent co-financing, which would be released only if the rise in prices for items in the budget for GCF proceeds exceeds a predetermined threshold.

* The project has a high economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 21.1 per cent, with long-term benefits far exceeding the initial investment, thereby providing confidence that the intervention will be sustained.

## Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### Environmental and social safeguards

* The project will involve the rehabilitation of coastal ecosystems and the capacity-building of the beneficiaries and government institutions to improve coping capacities in relation to climate change. These activities are expected to contribute towards (i) re-establishing/restoring the hydrological processes of wetlands through waterways restoration/clearing; (ii) rehabilitating mangrove and swamp forest/grassland; (iii) improving


---
**Page 84**
---

Freshwater-salifer recharge through dams, channels and infiltration wells, and (iv) effectively managing coastal ecosystems through scientific monitoring of key ecosystem indicators. These ground-based interventions will be undertaken in seven target sites within two coastal stretches of the Gulf of Mexico: (i) the Yucatan Peninsula; and (ii) the coast between Manzanillo, encompassing 2.0 million ha of land and marine areas. The project will be implemented by a consortium led by the Mexican branch of the Global Environment Facility and Environment (GTMA) and the Cuban branch of the Latin American Faculty of Social Sciences (FLASCO Cuba). The accredited entity (AE) has assessed the project to be of moderate risk, which is consistent with the GCF’s definition of moderate risk. The Secretariat of GTMA and Environment agrees with this categorization since although the project would be located in natural habitat areas, there are no significant risks associated with the project that could have a negative impact on the ecosystem services. The potential adverse environmental and social risks and/or impacts of the project (considered as moderate) can be effectively managed and mitigated and will generally be addressed by standard mitigation and management measures. The Secretariat confirms that the project is consistent with GCF’s ESAs.

Environmental and social safeguards (ESS) instruments. The project underwent the social and environmental screening procedure and environmental and social assessment (ESA) of the AE, which concluded that the project would have a moderate risk of adverse impacts and a multi-tiered grievance redress mechanism. A separate stakeholder consultation report and engagement plan have been developed.

Compliance with GCF ESS standards. Paragraphs 37–42 below summarize the key risks and impacts in relation to the GCF ESS standards and how they have been addressed in the proposal.


### ES 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The project proposes underlining the social and environmental screening procedure and ESA, which concluded that the project would have a moderate risk of adverse impacts. The seven target sites and the interventions that would be undertaken on each of those sites and has associated potential risks and impacts are described in detail below. The proposed project includes an ESMF, which provides generic management and mitigation measures for the risks and potential impacts of various ground-based interventions at the project sites. More specific environmental and social safeguards will be developed as part of the implementation.

The site-specific interventions will be subject to at least six key steps/processes: namely; (i) identification of the relevant ESS instruments, (ii) development of an ESMF, (iii) drafting preparation of an ESMF, (iv) preparation/issuance of environmental procedures, instructions and plans, (v) implementation of the project, and (vi) monitoring and evaluation of environmental inspection checklists; (c) corrective actions for any non-conformance with the above process.

### ES 2: Labour and working conditions

The project implementing units are likely to engage a few staff, researchers, aides and labourers. While small-scale civil works at the project sites would be undertaken by local contractors, the AE has developed an ESMF that includes analysis of the national legislation related to labour conditions and the prevailing labour practices in the region. The ESMF also provides guidance on how to ensure occupational health and safety concerns, as well as to guarantee appropriate labour and working conditions. The standard environmental contracts for closure of works will also include provisions for workers’ compensation and other benefits.

### ES 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

There is a potential for temporary and localized contamination of water channels in the form of increased turbidity.


---
**Page 85**
---

sedimentation and silt ing from minor civil works. This will be addressed by developing and implementing an erosion drainage and sediment control plan for any surface works, excavation work, water crossings and storm water pathways. The plan would include minimizing cleared areas, locating excavated material away from waterways, and stockpiling excavated materials away from waterways. The use of infiltration wells, which is intended to reduce the amount of runoff that may enter waterways, will also counter changes on the groundwater and coastal aquifer. Management measures for addressing surface and groundwater quality concerns have been identified in the ESMP. The groundwater quality will be monitored by the Ministry of Health.

### ES 6: Community health, safety and security.
The restoration of the ecosystem functions is expected to provide a range of benefits to local communities. However, there is a small possibility of adverse impacts on the health and safety of local communities due to possible short-term contamination of waterways during construction work. The project will be implemented in close coordination with the wetlands ecosystem. The ESA report provides baseline information on the prevalence of waterborne diseases in the area. The Ministry of Health has identified that the resources are not deemed sufficient to address any health and safety issues arising from the project. The Ministry of Health has recommended that a monitoring plan for water quality be developed wherein water quality is monitored through the network of water quality and hydrogeological monitoring stations. The project will also include the development of awareness-raising and education campaigns on the risk of waterborne diseases and will require contractors to strictly control the formation of standing water and accumulation of construction waste in areas where there are high concentrations of mosquitoes.

### ES 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement.
The ESA report has determined that no land acquisitions or involuntary resettlements will be required for project implementation. However, during the implementation of the project at the interventions will be undertaken on government-owned lands, including protected areas. The activities related to the project intervention will be carried out in close coordination with the relevant forestry and mangrove companies of the respective provinces and are under a societal State property regime. The presence of indigenous peoples or other vulnerable groups such as pastoralists and mangrove species was not identified during the consultations carried out as part of the formulation of this ESMP.

### ES 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources.
There is a possibility of an inadvertent introduction of invasive species in the rehabilitation of degraded areas. The presence of non-native plant species (either planted or already existing in the ecosystem) will proliferate and colonize areas more quickly than native plants, which may result in the loss of biodiversity. Such changes to ecosystem functions may indeed adversely affect critical natural habitats or compromise existing protected areas. To minimize adverse impacts on the environment for the proposed works, activities will be developed under an approved management plan. The ESMP will take into consideration the potential spread of weeds through erosion and sediment entering waterways and will include measures to control the spread of non-native species.

### ES 7: Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES 5: indigenous peoples.
The AFE provides for the screening of indigenous peoples in the ESA report and has determined that there are no indigenous peoples in Cuba. In any case, the project scope and activities pose minimal risks in relation to indigenous peoples.

### ES B: Cultural heritage.
The screening conducted indicates that the project will not adversely affect any cultural heritage structures or sites. The ESA report has identified potential sensitive areas of cultural heritage within the project area where construction work may impact those sites will be directly affected by the project. In the case of the discovery of archaeological


---
**Page 86**
---

sites and other objects of cultural and historical interests that may become apparent during project implementation, contractors are required to follow certain procedures, as set out in the environmental clauses of the contract agreements, including immediate cessation of work in the area, notification to the relevant authorities and reporting to the Green Climate Fund.

### Implementation arrangements

As the implementing agency, AMA, which is part of CITMA, will be responsible for the implementation of the ESMP via the delivery organisations (e.g. contractors) through a participatory approach that takes into account social and cultural issues and environmental and social monitoring and reporting. A safeguards manager will also be required to ensure compliance with the ESMP.

A field officer will be stationed at each project site, and an office in Havana. A Field officer will be responsible for day-to-day inspections at the project sites and will provide technical expertise on environmental and social issues as requested; an overall responsibility of the project team will be that with environmental and social safeguards issues also be undertaken.

#### Stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism

The project design has undergone extensive stakeholder consultations, and a detailed stakeholder engagement plan have been prepared. National and local consultations have been held involving representatives from institutions from the following sectors, among others: farming, forestry, livestock, fisheries, water resources management, environment, education, public health, science, technology, conservation management, civil defence, finance, economy and planning, as well as with government agencies, non-governmental organisations (NGOs) and regulatory and enforcement authorities (e.g. the Forest Rangers Corps). Various governmental and non-governmental stakeholders have been involved in the project design, including through meetings with the communities located in the project intervention areas.

The EIA report contains a sufficiently detailed description of a multi-tiered grievance redress mechanism that is consistent with the national legal framework for environmental matters at the provincial level (second tier), and within the accountability mechanism of the AE and existing nationally agreed grievance redress mechanisms. The head of the corrective programme management unit will be designated as the key officer in charge of the grievance redress mechanism.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The gender equality framework at the AE reflects the enabling environment and legal and institutional framework that provide the context for the promotion of gender equality and non-discrimination, including the Constitution of Cuba, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women and other international agreements strengthening women's rights and gender equality. The Constitution of Cuba recognises and guarantees the right to equal treatment in all areas of public life, and prohibits any discrimination against men or women. The National Plan for Gender Equality 2013-2017 (Plan 2030) is based on "human development, equity and social justice" and guarantees the rights and duties of both men and women equally. The AE has developed gender equality policies, laws and development plans in place; in practice there were cultural and social biases and institutional barriers to women's participation in decision-making processes. However, vulnerability in single-parent families with women at the head; the percentage of households headed by women stood at 46 per cent in 2012.

The gender equality framework at the AE based on desk reviews and stakeholder consultations demonstrates that Cuba is performing well in terms of female representation in legislative work, despite gaps in the productive sector. In certain sectors, the employment rate


---
**Page 87**
---

For women above or near equal to that for men. However, generally, Cuban women are not equally represented in decision-making in the private sector and remain underemployed and constrained by sociocultural barriers, reduced access to training and the burden of domestic and child-rearing responsibilities. In addition, women own less land than men, and more men than women are members of and hold managerial positions on cooperatives. This is due to a combination of traditional patriarchal norms and beliefs that recognize and value men's work, while undervaluing the role of women.

Although the promotion of women in the agricultural sector has been encouraged and the national government has taken steps to address gender inequality, women still constitute a minority in productive agricultural work as well as in management positions, in addition to not being represented equally among cooperatives. Women also have limited access to equipment of agricultural machinery. This further limits access to and representation of women among more "technical" roles within the sector. In addition, women are less likely than men to be part of the workforce, they are engaged in the less well remunerated activities, contrary to men, the majority of whom are involved in more productive activities (fishing and diving) with better access to more valuable fisheries resources.

### 52. Women's participation in the fisheries sector is primarily related to the processing of catch, as women constitute a large proportion of the workforce in this area. This represents another barrier for women that limits their ability to self-care, recreation and development initiatives. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making processes regarding traditional medicine and crafts, as a result of which they are more vulnerable to mangrove loss and salinity intrusion. The latter affects their household and family care roles, which depend on access to fresh water.

Women represent approximately 20 per cent of the workforce and while the number of women in decision-making positions is increasing, this remains insufficient for them to be able to influence policy decisions and support for women who have been victims of gender-based violence, there are no specific laws regarding their protection. The increase in sea levels has led to an increase in the number of deaths among women due to increased frequency and intensity of hurricanes and flooding. Women also face greater risks from climate change because they are more vulnerable to long-term impacts of climate change because of their more direct and limited access to and control of resources, economic vulnerability, and domestic and reproductive roles.

The GCF Gender Policy Action Plan includes activities that address the challenges identified in this section. The plan will include a gender action plan with specific targets, timelines and budgets. The AE will hire an ESS specialist with expertise in gender mainstreaming to support and guide the activities within the gender action plan and mainstream gender considerations into all other GCF-funded projects. Through consultations with women and women's groups, it will undertake additional consultations and will collect baseline data on women's participation in fisheries sector decision-making processes. Women-headed households, the elderly, young people and disabled persons are also being supported through awareness-raising and training on climate change impacts and mitigation measures.

The gender action plan will include activities that aim to increase women's access to opportunities and build capacity, as well as actions to support women's inclusion in decision-making both within the project itself and the institutions implementing the project. More specifically, it will address the challenges identified for women in the fisheries sector, as well as in the organizational structures associated with the implementation of the project. It will also consider how to ensure that women are included in all aspects of project design, leadership and decision-making (including marine and coastal wetlands monitoring; EBA activities; etc.). Women will be targeted as a specific group for awareness-raising on the use of such EBA approaches and will be the target group for awareness-raising on climate-based information products, while being equally represented in the development of proposals for the

The Secretariat has therefore decided to approve the application for accreditation of GCF in Cuba only if and when GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been granted a waiver from such obligations.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP157

## Proposal name: Coastal Resilience to Climate Change in Cuba through Ecosystem Based Adaptation - "MI COSTA"

### Accredited entity:
United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Country/ies:
Republic of Cuba

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential: Scale: High

Cuba is located in the Caribbean, which is a global hotspot for hurricane formation and subsequent landfall. The country is one of the small island developing states (SIDS). The island is elongated with its longest axis running east-west. The capital city, Havana, and the largest coastal city are located on the southern coasts. An estimated 57 per cent of the population of the country lives in these two cities. The coastline of Cuba is approximately 2400 km long and is subject to meteorological hazards such as hurricanes and associated storm surges and flooding. Moreover, sea level rise has been observed.

Due to a rise in sea surface temperature in recent decades, hurricane activity in the region has increased. There has also been a rise in the sea level along the coastal zones of Cuba resulting from climate change and land subsidence. The frequency of hurricanes has increased. Moreover, gradual salinization has been adversely affecting crop agriculture. The worst effect of rising sea levels is the inundation of low-lying areas, which results in loss of agricultural lands, which erodes the remaining beachfronts and limits recreational and tourism activities in the land areas affected.

In recent decades, hurricanes have struck Cuba's coasts with much increased intensity and frequency. Cuba was affected by 12 hurricanes between 2001 and 2017, 10 of which were category 3 or higher. The number of deaths due to these storms has been increasing. The cost affecting the country has risen from a historical average of 26 per cent to a staggering 78 per cent. This has caused a significant rise in the loss burden. The projection on beachfront inundation for the period between 1950 and 2030 is estimated at 4,000 ha (15 sq km) and 440,540 ha, respectively. The rate of loss of beach due to inundation induced as sea level rise has been increasing.

The Government of Cuba has been keen on providing necessary support during post-hurricane emergency periods and for rehabilitation of communities. The need for mobilization of hard currency for these purposes is high. However, the country's dependence on global economies has been increasing. Unless a solution is found, people will continue to struggle under (climate) change.

In addition to the increased hurricane activity induced by climate change, Cuba's coasts have experienced an increase in the occurrence of moderate to strong floods. These floods are associated with heavy rainfall and storm surges. The frequency of these events has been linked to sea level. To add to this, warm Pacific El Niño events have led to an increase in extra-tropical storms, with associated risk of inundation of coastlines, including the coastal municipalities of (Cuba).


---
**Page 94**
---

### Medium to High

#### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

The funding proposal addresses two major outputs: (i) promotion of ecosystem services in the face of climate impacts; and (ii) promotion of a shift from a traditional reactive (disaster risk reduction) approach to a proactive planned approach. The latter approach is to be complemented by planning, institutional strengthening, and capacity-building.


---
**Page 95**
---

While partners have confirmed that EBA has multifaceted beneficial impacts in terms of reducing adverse impacts of climate-induced effects, the second output highlights the potential for causing a shift from the current paradigm to embrace the new resilient paradigm by promoting an ecosystem-centred holistic planning approach with improved international oversight and guidance.

12. The evidence from Cuba, including the recently completed Manglar Yoo project (financed by the Green Climate Fund), indicates that EBA is a cost-effective alternative to structural approaches for coastal protection against storm surge. Moreover, additional benefits likely to accrue from mangrove restoration include increased biodiversity, improved ecosystem resilience building efforts through mangrove restoration. Such restoration, along with other climate change adaptation measures, can be implemented in Cuba and elsewhere and could be replicated within Cuba, and perhaps throughout the Caribbean islands where possible. Instead of locking in large amounts of funding, the project will provide a cost-effective means to carbon sequestration through mangrove restoration, as well as providing ancillary beneficial effects, including increased resilience and enhancement of biodiversity by symbiotic relationships with mangroves.

13. The project will also generate new EBA-related data for information replication opportunities, which in turn will generate EBA-related data for information replication among other parts of Cuba's coastline, and for regional and global communities to learn and emulate in their circumstances. The northern coast of Cuba is the most vulnerable part of the country to climate change impacts; however, the project should also focus on sharing such knowledge with other established coastal countries in the Caribbean region. The Technical Assistance and Policy Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) will leverage the clarification from the accredited entity, in response to a question posed by the TAP, regarding the role of EBA within the context of the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) will mobilize its networks of experts, and use its participation in various regional and global climate change initiatives to ensure that the lessons learned are shared in Cuba through the GCF-supported project. Moreover, the evidence collected by the project will be packaged into a series of tools and guidelines for replication and knowledge management, channelled and disseminated at relevant events, forums and meetings within the Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre.

14. The exchange of information sharing and learning will be further realized through the implementation of the capacity-building programme. The latter will entail: (i) development of tools and techniques based on lessons learned from experience; (ii) routine compilation of data, evaluation of management practices and knowledge; and their dissemination; (iii) the consolidation of results in the form of toolskits and guidelines that will be designed for various stakeholders to use as a reference point for climate change planning and adaptation, where local level knowledge will be matched and complemented with institutional capacity. The project will also provide opportunities for training workshops on climate change planning to take note of natural dynamics and mainstream ecosystem-based territorial planning in the context of climate change. The project will also generate evidence of climate change through the implementation of the project and the monitoring mechanisms within it.

15. Based on the above-mentioned capacity-building activities, the project will create opportunities for local stakeholders to engage with the national government. There is provision for advice on regulatory aspects for the local municipalities, which is expected to strengthen their ability to implement EBA in a manner that is consistent and coherent to avoid further degradation of red mangroves. As envisaged, the project will contribute to the creation of a new generation of coastal managers at the municipal level. The trained municipal-level personnel will benefit from ongoing local stakeholders to ensure such planning by focusing on fish production and other livelihoods. It is expected that the likelihood of adoption of a legal provision for EBA will favour evidence-based learning and subsequent

# Sustainable development potential

## Scale: High

### 13. Sustainable development potential

The proposed project is expected to directly contribute some of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):

- **SDG 5 (gender equity)** – by addressing gender concerns in an effort to build resilience, giving women better opportunities to protect lives and ensuring well-being in the event of a hurricane.
- **SDG 13 (enhanced climate action)** – by taking proactive measures to reduce vulnerabilities of coastal populations of Cuba; and
- **SDG 6 (clean water)** – by providing coastal wetlands and creating opportunities for mangroves to thrive.

In addition to the above-mentioned direct contributions to SDGs, the project will indirectly contribute to most of them. The project will contribute directly to safe water, and also to SDG 13 by offering water cleaning co-benefits along the coastal aquifer systems in Cuba. The project is aligned with the National Climate Change Strategy and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030, where the EBA approach will address people's vulnerability to hurricanes and associated issues of storm surges and floods.

Furthermore, the implementation of planned coastal development intended by climate risk will enable the communities in the target municipalities to reduce their exposure to risks from hurricanes, storm surges, flooding, saline intrusion and other phenomena. Based on the experiences from the baseline project Magpie Valley, it is estimated that mangrove restoration alone in that case helped reduce flood damage by 50%. The implementation of planned coastal development will further reduce vulnerability of the affected communities is expected to reduce the need for mobilization of finance by the Government of Cuba for emergency assistance and post-disaster recovery. This


---
**Page 97**
---

saving is estimated in the range of from USD 97 million to USD 278 million per disaster. Therefore, one may expect that the project will provide significant economic benefits for both communities as well as the Government of Cuba.

### Social co-benefits: Coastal fisheries, availability of non-timber products, reduction in salinity and water quality improvement, and improved health are all social co-benefits anticipated from the reduction in salinity. The improvement in water quality will also add to the same health objective. The regeneration of mangroves close to municipalities will enhance possibilities for fishing and other livelihoods activities. In addition, the project will provide additional well-being amenities to the inhabitants. Mangrove restoration will reduce the current rate of erosion by 50% and increase the resilience of coastal communities against flooding. This will allow access to beaches in the intervention areas and allow continuation of recreational services for the communities.

### Environmental co-benefits: Ecosystem restoration is the central theme for the project approach. The project will contribute significantly towards the achievement of both terrestrial and environmental aspects. The project aims to reduce mangrove degradation, which it will deliver through the establishment of a network of protected areas and the creation of enhanced conditions of both terrestrial and aquatic biodiversity. It is expected that, due to project-related improvements in water quality, coastal wetlands such as seagrass beds and mangroves, Seagrass restoration will contribute towards improving the health of coastal wetlands.

### Gender equality: Resilience-building by means of enhanced physical protection against extreme weather events and through the implementation of climate change adaptation measures reduce pre- and post-hazard stress of women and significantly reduce mortality rates. Soil conservation and water management practices, such as the use of sandbags for the purpose of salinity reduction, increased freshwater infiltration into groundwater aquifers, greater biodiversity, and increased availability of non-timber forest products from regenerated mangroves will also provide significant benefits to women. Women in coastal communities are likely to experience these benefits directly, given the gender-differentiated roles of men and women at the household and community levels. The potential health and well-being benefits will facilitate


---
**Page 98**
---

The achievement of gender equity. The project presents a gender action plan, with due emphasis on actions that will contribute to gender equity.

Based on the available information presented, it appears that the project's sustainable development potential is high.


### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

1. Cuba is one of the SIDS. Its location in the Caribbean makes it subject to hurricanes, as the South Atlantic region is one of the most active hurricane regions in the world. In recent decades, Cuba has experienced a significant increase in the frequency and intensity of hurricanes. Both the south-west and south-east coast zones of the country are known hotspots for hurricanes. The impacts of these natural disasters include damage to buildings, physical infrastructure, and disrupt critical services such as drinking water supply and electricity in the aftermath of hurricane events. In 2017 alone, Cuba received US$35 million from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for emergency assistance and restoration of livelihoods in the order of US$97 million to USD 2.8 billion in total since 2014. The country has also invested heavily in infrastructure projects to mitigate climate impacts on the nation's natural capital and public services. However, many of these have unintentionally contributed to further degrading coastal landscapes. Elba represents an innovative approach that seeks to address this issue by integrating climate change adaptation and socioeconomic development. Cuba’s expertise can allow this approach to be scaled up in other vulnerable countries.

2. However, there are gaps in technological capacity, information and monitoring equipment that are beyond the scope of the project. The country has also experienced a significant decline as a result of the decades-long trade embargo. As a result, it has become extremely difficult to ensure that people have access to the basic needs and services required for the provision of Cuba. Therefore, the needs of the recipient in the wake of hurricanes, storm surges and flooding driven by climate change are paramount.

3. - Cuba’s population is 11.2 million distributed in 15 provinces and 146 municipalities.
   - The per capita gross domestic product of Cuba was USD $822 in 2018, with a growth rate of 7% over the past five years.
   - The country has one of the highest levels of income inequality and extreme poverty. The country’s human development index stands at 0.615 ranking Cuba seventeenth out of 189 countries in 2019. As at 2012, Cuba's public debt was 33.5 per cent of gross domestic product.
   - The majority of the population lives in rural areas and small municipalities are limited, accounting for only about 30 per cent of total employment. Therefore, the major economic activities are concentrated in urban areas.

4. Recently, Cuba has embraced upon ecosystem-based approaches, and piloted EBA in a recently concluded project titled Manglar Viva, aligned with Tarea Vida. This has given a first opportunity to assess the potential for ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) as opposed to the latter having adverse environmental externalities. From this pilot project, it appears evident that EbA can be an effective and cost-effective approach in addressing climate change driven-impacts.
   - The recently collected data have been cross-checked with community representatives at the municipality level through consultative processes which formed the basis of the project.

5. Because of the long-term political embargo, Cuba has been forced to accept economic indicators that are not comparable to those of other countries and to miss out on trade opportunities, despite having comparative advantages in producing specific commodities for global trade. The embargo has also isolated Cuba from the rest of the world, which has given rise to artificial barriers to taking active measures to reduce poverty vulnerability. Only in December 2020 this Cuban peso was introduced into systems with the global currency first time since 1960 and experienced a sudden ten-fold decline in the value of the Cuban peso with respect to the United States dollar. Under such economic

# Country ownership

## Cuba acknowledges the importance of coastal protection and protection of mangroves as measures to protect its communities affected by climate change. Cuba's National Environmental Policy (2015) recognizes the role of mangroves in carbon sequestration and their contribution to national adaptation, and has developed guidelines for economic and social policy where the importance of access to water resources is highlighted. The country has also developed sectoral policies that well in terms of coastal protection, while ecosystem restoration and augmentation receive due importance in relation to climate change mitigation.

## Scale: High

### 1. Country ownership

The project is well grounded within the governance structure of the country. Actions and interventions are integrated into the government structures, where local actors and institutions participate throughout planning and implementation of activities, so that maximum impact can be achieved. The role of the national environment agency is recognized in the project design, and their involvement is given due priority towards implementation. Cuba's Agencia de Medio Ambiente (Environment Agency) has been chosen as the executing entity in a bid to ensure country ownership.

20. The officially designated authority for Cuba to coordinate with GCF is the Ministry of Science, Technology and Environment (CITMA) - the ministry in charge of affairs on policy coordination, control and executing activities relating to science, technology and environment.
The Environmental Agency of Cuba (AE), as a member of CITMA, will be responsible on behalf of CITMA, which is the appropriate body mandated to promote EBA in Cuba. Under the direction of AE, the project will be implemented through the Cuban Institute for Marine Environmental Management through research and implementation of strategic projects and by putting into practice the results obtained from these projects.
The project will cover the purchase of equipment required for the execution of the environmental management of the marine environment as well as the maintenance costs. The Government of Cuba has run its affairs, which will be used to cover the annual operation and maintenance costs for the equipment. The project is expected to be implemented through a public-private partnership with the marine environment.

a. UNDP is the implementing entity for the project. UNDP has a strong presence in Cuba. It leverages expertise in capacity-building, provides technical and policy support, addresses barriers, and creates enabling conditions for adaptation planning and investments. The UNDP local office in Havana has been involved in the implementation of several projects that have been implemented globally. Moreover, UNDP Cuba has been involved in the implementation of several projects related to climate change adaptation, including disaster risk reduction, sustainable natural resources management, and increasing resilience at various levels. The expertise and experience of UNDP will be leveraged for the successful implementation of the project.

There were also consultations at the subnational and national levels involving institutions representing different sectors such as agriculture, health, education, and environment. The Interministerial Coordination Committee of Cuba was duly apprised of the project design before the project was approved by GCF. The extensive process of project design gives confidence in the quality of the project by the stakeholders.

c. Although there is no specific mention of the involvement of women's groups in the consultations, the inclusion of gender equality and the empowerment of women as a methodology in the gender action plan provide assurance that gender-related concerns are considered in the design of the project.
d. From the above analysis, the independent TAP is of opinion that the project’s country ownership is high.

a. The Government of Cuba is offering or financing of USD 26.3 million as co-financing for this project. This shows that there is a high level of government commitment given the context.

b. GCF has been requested to fund as the proponents have provided evidence that the EBA activities are economically more cost-effective than the other alternative engineering-based option - hard protection (involving sea walls, etc.). Moreover, the goods and services generated through the project will be used for the benefit of the Cuban population. The benefits that will be generated and private financing will need to come in the foreseeable future; moreover,

### Project will not lock in large cash flow in infrastructure

#### The project will not lock in large cash flow in infrastructure. Rather, it will allow both the community and the government to save hard cash by reducing the loss burden from repeated high-intensity hazards. In addition, it will allow the regeneration and restoration of mangrove protection areas that have been lost due to climate change.

The sequestration potential has not been considered as the key benefit, but it will certainly be effective in the long term.

#### The proponents have checked the economic efficiency of the project. The predicted annualized cost savings of having protective infrastructure in place have been compared against project costs. The higher the intensity of the event, the greater the loss burden precipitated. Very high intensity events are rare (less than 10 per cent) but they can be catastrophic. For example, for events that occur either in 50 years or even once in 100 years. Based on frequency analysis and historical data, it is estimated that the project will save USD 28 million compared to what would have been avoided due to (mangrove) protection were estimated - and used for the economic efficiency analysis. A discount rate of 10 per cent was considered for the economic analysis. Outputs from the economic analysis show a net present value (NPV) of USD 356 million, an internal rate of return (IRR) of 24 per cent, taking into account the potential for carbon capture. The NPV for a 30-year period is estimated at USD 1 billion. This is based on the assumption that there will be no "no intervention" analysis; the project's baseline NPV stands at USD 100 million, with an IRR of 24 per cent.

#### The economic performance of the project should ideally take into consideration a scenario without the project, so that a rational comparison may be examined. The “without the project” scenarios show that in the absence of the project, the baseline NPV would result in a loss of 4,600 ha of mangrove areas and an additional loss USD 5.2 million. This is because the project will reduce the risk of erosion and sedimentation decrease in project benefits. The base case economic model results in a negative NPV of -USD 38 million and an IRR of 1 per cent, below the 10 per cent return threshold. Sensitivity analysis used in this study shows that if the baseline NPV is positive at USD 64.8 million. The sensitivity analysis shows that a 20 per cent increase in costs (for instance, as a result of increased maintenance cost) would make the NPV negative at -USD 66.8 million. As a result of negative externalities on the ecosystem) will push the EIR to 0.7 per cent. Any further increase in costs would make the project unviable and lead to loss of mangrove territory. The NPV is negative in all scenarios. The comparison proves the sound approach being sought by the proponents, which is based on the fact that the project will reduce maintenance cost of EIA interventions (which require capacities easily found within Cauh) compared with a reliance on off-site infrastructure and equipment heavy investments.

#### In conclusion, the project will not lock in large cash flow in infrastructure. Rather, it will allow both the community and the government to save hard cash by reducing the loss burden from repeated high-intensity hurricanes that could occur. In that event, part of the mangrove restoration and regeneration efforts would be made possible through the proposed mangrove plantations. In most cases, the reinvestment requirements cannot be made through an ongoing project. In the proposed project, the mangrove regeneration efforts will be brought under an insurance service, where government will pay an estimated USD 5 million in premiums in the


---
**Page 102**
---

initial years of the project. In the event that a hurricane event destroys the nurtured mangrove areas, the payout from the insurance will cover the cost of replanting, and thereby help achieve the long-term sustainability of the mangroves. The independent TAP finds the considerable payment for insurance to be justified.

In conclusion, if the project is implemented as planned and during implementation, there will be ensured protection that would cover over the damage to the mangroves.


### Cuba has much experience in managing its coastal resources, including mangroves. Its institutions are eager to emulate global best practices, a few of which have been integrated into the project design. However, the success of this project is highly dependent on the ability of the institutions need to engage strongly with the local communities to discourage any anthropogenic activities that could lead to destruction of the mangrove areas and also to check a range of unsustainable practices carried out to date. If human-led destruction cannot be checked, the effectiveness of mangrove restoration and long-term protection will only be theoretical. Therefore, the rapid implementation approach to emulating upon global best-mangrove management practices must receive high priority along with the policy and legal frameworks. Otherwise, the ultimate success of this project is highly questionable.


### In view of the above considerations, the independent TAP finds the efficiency and effectiveness of the project to be medium to high.

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project.

The independent TAP also recommends the following:

* The project management unit, guided by the national project steering committee, should engage with the local communities in a participatory manner through all project processes, including through the project's planned provincial coordination and municipal coordination structures, so that the communities are aware of their role in protecting mangroves from activities that could potentially damage them. The project must pay additional attention to social engagement so that business-as-usual degradation or destruction of mangroves is reversed, with active involvement of community members and a behavioral change orchestrated through greater community engagement.

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## Introduction

The Green Climate Fund recognizes the central importance of gender considerations in terms of both impact and access to climate funding and requires a Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAAP) as part of its project proposals that it assesses. The main objective of the Gender Assessment is to screen the gender dimensions of the project, and to subsequently strengthen the gender responsive actions within the project.

In particular, this gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the issue of gender in Cuba, with a specific focus on the gender differentiated impacts of climate change. This information will be used as input for the design of a gender-sensitive Project. By identifying gender issues relevant to the Project interventions and situated in the Cuban context, across elements such as use and access to resources, gender-differentiated roles in employment, and decision-making processes, this Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAAP) identifies opportunities for gender mainstreaming in the design of the "Coastal Adaptation to Climate Change in Cuba through Ecosystem Based Adaptation – MIL COSTA project". The GAAP will also seek to address gendered climate resilience across the household, community and institutional levels.

The information in the present assessment is based on an analysis of Cuban social policies, programmatic documents, and statistics as well as academic and scientific papers and results, as well as interviews with key informants, focus group discussions with actors involved and Cuban beneficiaries living in the project intervention areas. The GAAP was also carried out with the support of the "Facultad Latinoamericana de Ciencias Sociales" (FLACSO) - Cuba program. The GAAP was developed by a team of experts from the GCF Secretariat and FLACSO team, comprised of experts specialized in socio-economic research, vulnerability, and gender equality, as well as community engagement. The principle objectives the GAAP are as follows:

1. To align the project proposal with Cuba's national priorities on gender.
2. To incorporate information and lessons learned from previous national gender studies and experiences in Cuba to both understand the context and ensure that project activities are gender responsive.
3. To present results from stakeholder consultations and involving women, men, girls and boys residing in the areas where the project will be developed from an early stage in project design.
4. To integrate gender considerations into project indicators, goals and activities, and identify those to which women can act as leaders and decision-makers in community-based adaptation interventions.

# ANNEX VIII - Gender Analysis and Action Plan

### Part I: Gender Analysis/Assessment Guide (Project/Program Level)

#### a) Methodology

The present gender assessment, carried out at the beginning of the design of this project, acts as an entry point for the incorporation of the gender perspective throughout the design and implementation phases. The analysis was based on the results of two rounds of stakeholder consultations, led by EAFOD, taking place in July and August 2018 in La Coloma (Pinar del Río), Playa Caño (Antemira) and Jicaro (Ciego de Ávila). The results of the consultations are detailed in the Stakeholder Consultation Report. The gender analysis framework presented below, as well as that presented in Section 2 of the present report has been designed by taking into account both the comprehensive gender assessment presented in the current section 1a and as well as the results of the consultations.

To summarize, the gender analysis and stakeholder participation and consultation, enabled the following:

- A strong participative process and accordingly a commitment by both local government officials and community members in fulfilling the objectives of the "Adapting to climate change in Cuba through Ecosystem Based Adaptation – Mi COSTA project"
- Demonstration of the need for gender-disaggregated data and indicators to establish a baseline in which to measure improvements and identify areas of interest; and
- Establishment of recommendations to be incorporated into the Gender Action Plan.

#### b) The Need for a Gender Perspective in Communities

Extensive literature and global experience has shown that women and men are often affected differently by the same socio-environmental conditions, and that depending on cultural norms and beliefs, power imbalances and differentiated roles in the household and public spheres, women and men have different capacities to adapt. The literature is also widely documented that women experience the effects of climate change differently than men, both in terms of adjusting livelihood strategies, in their changing relationships to scarce resources and in responding to environmental changes. Women are often responsible for family care tasks and decision-making power, which generally position women more in vulnerable situations.

It is therefore necessary to introduce a gender equity approach to project design, in order to implement the Gender Action Plan (GAAP). It is also important to understand that it is essential to understand that although women may be more vulnerable to the impacts of changing environmental conditions, the GAAP aims to understand the gendered differences in impacts between both men, women, girls and boys. For example, in the Cuban context, men can also


---
**Page 109**
---

face greater risks in disaster situations, due to the socially mandated roles they are expected to assume (embodiment characteristics such as strong, courageous, and decisive), which frequently leads to increased incidence of injuries and higher mortality rates.

### c) Existing Gender Inequality and Social Inclusion in Cuba

Cuba society demonstrates exceptional gender equality, despite the fact that, like other countries, Cuba remains broadly patriarchal, with certain manifestations of gender inequality mainly affecting women and girls. Although the Cuban Revolution of 1959 set out to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women, it was not until the 2004 Constitution that a series of political transformations created a unique situation of equity among its citizens, the cultural stigmas and stereotypes placing women in some areas disadvantaged positions have tended to prevail. In recent years, Cuba has made significant progress with regard to the participation of women in the economic and social development spheres of Cuba; more work remains to be done, signalled in recent speeches by Raúl Castro himself, the former commander-in-chief of the Cuban Revolution. At the 2015 United Nations Commission on the Status of Women, addressed the UN at its “Global Leaders’ Meeting” on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment: A Commitment to Action”. He ended his speech by saying that “… political will is more important than resources; we must take the results obtained or try into our goals instead of making them a starting point and a pledge to achieve true equality for women”.

#### Gender Inequality Indicators

Several indices have been developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality, whereby the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and the Gender-related Development Index (GRDI). The GII is based on three important aspects of human development—reproductive health, empowerment and labour market participation. The GRDI accounts for disparities between women and men in three basic dimensions of human development—health, education and living standards.

According to the UNDP Assessment Report 2015, Cuba had a GII value of 0.304 (below the value of Latin America), which has positioned Cuba as the “62nd” in the world ranking. Cuba’s GRDI value, in the report was listed at 0.946, placing them in Group “3” out of five groups, which is defined as “countries with medium equality in HDI achievements between women and men”.

Another useful indicator is the World Economic Forum’s (WEF) Global Gender Gap Index, which analyses the division of resources and opportunities between men and women in 144 countries. It measures the size of the gender inequality gap in four key areas: health and survival, education attainment, economic participation and opportunity and political

Summarized below are national statistics and references to the political and legal framework of Cuba, which contribute to the country's gender equity conditions nationally, specifically in relation to the issues identified for both causes of discrimination against women, making them more vulnerable to climate change:

1) **Division of labour**, lower income and lesser livelihood opportunities for women:
   - 49% of the labour force in the civil state sector is female
   - 48.6 % of managers in Cuba are women

2) Fewer legal rights:
   - Law 116 "Labour Code", Article 2, establishes Equality at Work as one of the fundamental principles governing labour law: "Every citizen in a position to work has the right to obtain employment in accordance with the demands of the economy and at his or her choice, whether he is male or female, regardless of race, colour, religion, sex, age, on the basis of skin colour, gender, religious beliefs, sexual orientation, territorial origin, disability, or any other distinction prejudicial to human dignity."
   - The Bases of the Economic and Social Development Plan 2010-2030, in the Strategic Axis "Human Development Equity and Social Justice", establishes the specific objective 19: "To guarantee the exercise of the rights and duties of all citizens, with equality, inclusion and social justice."

3) Lesser political and professional representation:

In Cuba, women represent:

These factors are further elaborated in the following sections.
* Poverty

One of the priorities of the government, since the Cuban Revolution of 1959 was the eradication of poverty in Cuba. To a large extent, the post-revolution socio-economic transformation and social policies were able to reverse many of the structural issues responsible for poverty.

"It should be noted that during the sixties, seventies and first half of the eighties important advances were made, fundamentally in health, education and social security, and the trend towards greater equality was evident. This is reflected in the indicators used, as evidenced by the sustained increase in the income received by the poorest population"1.

One of the indicators of this process has been the decrease in the Gini coefficient from 1953 to the end of the 1980s, demonstrating the reduction of inequality in Cuban society. However, the tightening of the economic and financial embargo imposed by the United States as well as the deterioration of the terms of trade led to a significant decline in the country's productive capacity, which in turn led to a gradual but steady deterioration of the living conditions of the Cuban population. The equity gaps widened, and the government's ability to reduce poverty was severely limited. Although poverty had not been totally eradicated, it was a deterioration of the mechanisms for its relief2.

Since then the situation has considerably improved and it is important to note that in Cuba, poverty has a distinct character; given that all citizens are guaranteed access to basic social

services such as health, education and employment. That is, unlike elsewhere, the poverty in Cuba is poverty with protection, with comprehensive social security services provided by the State based on a universal access approach. That is why extreme situations of poverty, such as hunger, or high mortality rates are not present*. Accordingly, in Cuban academic literature and discourse, the term “poverty” refers to the vulnerability of certain groups at risk-populations are used. These groups include the following:

- Female-headed households.
- Low-income households.
- Households with members with disabilities.
- Households with older adults living alone.
- Households exposed to environmental risks (e.g. close to the sea/coastal areas).

That is, despite the relatively high level of gender equality, the socio-demographic profile of the most vulnerable population in Cuba, has a higher frequency of female-headed households: headed by women who are single mothers and/or whose children’s needs, dependent or are in a precarious situation in the labour market due to their low income. Furthermore, studies such as those by Pérez (2013) and Zabaleta (2015) show important differences between men and women within poor households, which places women at a greater disadvantage than men.*

Overall, the segment of the population that faces the most vulnerable socioeconomic conditions is single-parent families with women at the head.* According to census data, this segment increased from 3.8 percent in 1981 to 44.9 percent in 2012.

### Health

Since the Cuban Revolution, health has been a key sector prioritized by the State, putting Cuba in an exceptional position among other countries in the region, obtaining health outcomes that are comparable with those of developed countries and contributing significantly to economic development. In the development of the health sector, women have played an important role, representing 73.0% of the 492,566 workers in the National Public Health System. They represent a significant proportion of the human resources available: 81.9% of doctors, with 81.9 doctors per 10,000 inhabitants.* There are 150 hospitals, 450 polyclinics, 131 maternity homes, 13 research institutes and comprehensive rehabilitation services at all

levels of care. The following three programmes are prioritised in the country: Maternal and Child Health, Comprehensive Care for the Elderly, and HIV/AIDS.

Several gender-focused prevention and health-care programmes have been implemented and developed. Women’s sexual and reproductive health is guaranteed, and the maternal mortality rate is 22 per 100,000 live births. The health system is integrated into the family and massive screening tests are carried out to detect breast cancer, breast cancer and other diseases at an early stage. The Maternal and Child Health Project (PMAM) gives special attention to early pregnancy, the Maternity and Conscious Parenthood Programme (Programa de Maternidad y Conciencia Paternidad Consciente), prevention programmes for HIV, STIs, AIDS and care for the elderly.

Cuba has a demographic context of low population growth, with a birth rate of 10.2 live births per 1000 inhabitants, a general fertility rate of 43.0 live births per 1000 women aged 15 to 49, and low mortality levels of 1.62 children per 1000 women. The overall mortality rate is 9.5 deaths/1000 population, with cardiovascular diseases (primarily heart disease) as the main cause of death. Male mortality is higher than female mortality except for in the case of cancer and accidents. Cuba has a low life expectancy at birth compared to other regions in regards to its population curve being significant skewed towards the elderly, rather than youth, with 20.1 percent of its population over 60 years of age[1], making the aging population more vulnerable to diseases such as cardiovascular disease. Life expectancy is generally high at over 78 years (76.5 years for men and 80.4 years for women)[1].

The Infant Mortality Rate has for ten consecutive years remained below five deaths under one year of age per 1000 live births, and in 2017 it was 4.0, the lowest in history[1]. The Maternal Mortality Ratio is 35/100,000 live births, with a high percentage due to obstetric causes (mainly due to complications related to the puerperium and others). In 2017, 29.4 induced abortions were performed for every 1000 women between 12 and 45 years of age[2], and 72.4 percent of these were in the first trimester of pregnancy[3]. The vaccination programme protects against 13 diseases, with 100 percent vaccination coverage, in all population groups, with no gendered differences. The impact indicators showing the elimination of mother-to-child transmission of HIV and congenital syphilis, certified by PAHO/WHO in June 2015, are also still met.
