# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL

Cuba’s employment policy since 1959 has been based on the principles of guaranteed employment, which translates into full employment for all citizens, the principle of equality and non-discrimination on the grounds of skin colour, sex or religious belief; and the principle of harmony between communal and individual interests. The legal framework relating to employment is comprehensive and well developed.

The Cuban state’s commitment to gender equality is strong: in practice, Cuban women remain overloaded with domestic and childcare responsibilities and are underrepresented in higher education, professional training, reducing women's chances of gaining access to more complete leadership positions (e.g., the “Fidel” model). Elsewhere, Cuban women face high work burdens including managing the demands of domestic life. Women also have a greater share than men of the most precarious jobs and activities that are considered 'women’s work' such as the making of handicrafts, canning and raising small livestock. This indicates the dominance of hegemonic masculinities that limit participation in paid employment.

Although the promotion of women in agriculture has been encouraged, national statistics reveal the continued presence of the agricultural sector leading to the minority presence of women in productive work as well as in management positions. The presence of the traditional social division of labour in the agricultural context means that women are not considered as equal partners with men and are often relegated to manual tasks due to the operation of agricultural machinery, which has led to less access and representation among these types of jobs.

In 2015, the Cuban National Statistics Office recorded that women employed in rural areas accounted for 37.6% of the total number of Cuban women engaged in paid work and 22.8% of the total number of rural women. They accounted for only 9.4% of agricultural workers in Cuba.

Furthermore, it is estimated that for every 100 men employed in rural areas, there are only 30 women employed. The women participation in the Cuban agricultural sector has been between 25 and 30% since 2006; in 2010 were 792,000 employed, 137,900 of them in this sector, 140,900 of then were women; in 2020 there were 792,000 employed, 137,900 of them.

Most managers have limited awareness of the need to employ women and the ways to offer them jobs (that is to create new jobs and create appropriate conditions for existing ones). There are also gender differences in the distribution of women in management positions, and many women tend to have responsibilities related more to the administrative tasks rather than those that require decision-making. This has led to recent initiatives by the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, the Federation of Cuban Women to promote gender equality in their management models.

Outside of the agricultural sector, women in Cuba show strong representation in the labour force, out-representing men in several. The following figures reflect this:

- 48% of the total number of people employed in the civil state sector
- 32% of the non-state sector
- 46% of the private employment
- 78.5% of health personnel
- 48% of scientific researchers
- 60.4% of holders of higher technical and professional qualification
- 65.2% of higher education graduates.[21]

### Education

Cuba is considered to have the best education system in Latin America, and one of the best in the world, particularly in regard to access and attainment of higher degrees. Education in Cuba is free for all citizens from birth until age 18 years old. The Cuban Revolution[22] included the expansion of the number of schools, the nationalization of private schools, a literacy campaign in 1961, which reached the most remote areas of the country, and the establishment of the National Institute of Higher Education (INES) in 1970. In 1980, a new constitution was approved, outlawing discrimination in all its forms, including gender discrimination, and established that in all state institutions the principle of equality would be

promoted and that all citizens should "enjoy education in all the country's educational institutions, from primary school to universities, which are state-owned."

The Cuban education system is organized into three-term levels: primary (preschool, primary school), secondary (basic secondary, pre-university, professional technical) and tertiary (doctoral level). Primary and basic secondary education is compulsory nationwide and most of Cuba's population has access to this type of education. In the 2017-2018 school year, 2,038,413 students were enrolled at all levels of education, 1,016,358 of whom were girls, accounting for 50 per cent of total enrollment. Cuba has a net enrolment rate in primary education of 97.5 per cent and the gross enrolment rates are 124.5 per cent at the level of the primary of 99.2 per cent, while at the lower secondary level it is 93.5 per cent[1]. The highest level of education attainment, the tertiary level, had an enrolment of 178,643 persons in the 2017-2018 school year. Of these students, 55.5% were women and 44.5% men; medical sciences (76,329), pedagogy (45,442), and technical sciences (30,870). An impressive sixty-two per cent of those enrolled in higher education in those fields were women[1]. There are however gender differences at the tertiary level. Women dominate in medicine, nursing, teaching and graduating from careers that have traditionally been recognized as "feminine", such as areas of social work, early childhood education, pedagogy, journalism and the arts and a lower representation in certain technical and professional fields.

Not only does Cuba's educational system demonstrate exceptional gender parity, it also addresses possible issues of intersectional marginalization with a special education programme for persons with disabilities. This program has the same curriculum as the General Education Programme but is adapted to meet their specific needs[2]. There are also programmes for Adult Education through adapted programs such as the Facultades Obreras Campesinas (Rural Workers' Colleges) and the Cursos para Trabajadores (Courses for Workers)[3].

#### Political Participation and Decision Making

The equal political participation of women in Cuba has also been one of the goals of the revolutionary government since 1959. This goal is aimed at a suite of actions have been implemented to achieve a greater presence of women in public life and to promote their increased contribution to the economic and social development of the country. These include


---
**Page 118**
---

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL

### The Literacy Campaign, the creation of the Federation of Cuban Women and the growing development of higher education.

In the last six decades, the participation of Cuban women in public and parliamentary life has been growing, which is expressed in the current composition of the National Assembly of People's Power (2018) with more than 53% female representation. These statistics place Cuba second in the world with the highest proportion of women in legislative work, surpassed only by Rwanda.

### Women’s participation in decision-making is increasing in Cuba

Not only has there been an increase in the presence of women in Parliament, but also a significant rise in their presence in the Council of State and the Council of Ministers. Although they do not represent a majority in these bodies, it should be noted that in recent years, more and more women are occupying leadership positions in Cuba in institutes, economic institutions, and service companies. However, their high levels of education often do not correspond to the positions they occupy.

In the political sphere, women have been present since the beginning of the Cuban Revolution. In today's Cuban society, there are 8 women ministers (Ministry of Food Industry; Ministry of Tourism; Ministry of Foreign Trade and Cooperation; Ministry of Labour and Social Security; Ministry of Science, Technology and Environment; Central Bank of Cuba and the Comptroller’s Office of the Republic) and 40 women deputy ministers. The Political Bureau of the Communist Party of Cuba has 12 female members (of which 8 are women [65.32 per cent]). Of the 612 members of the National Assembly of People's Power, 299 are women (48.86 per cent) and 313 are men (51.14 per cent).

In regard to the design and implementation of the project, it should also be borne in mind that women are well represented at all levels of decision-making within the Ministry of Agriculture, including the Minister of Science, Technology and Environment of Cuba (CITMA), the body responsible for "Tarea Vida" (Cuba's climate change strategy), the director of FLACSO (the national body responsible for the project), and the director of ICMAR (a key partner in capacity building and awareness raising), as well as the project design lead within ICMAR are all women.

### Access to Resources

In Cuba, both men and women have equal rights in access to resources (land, property goods, etc.). With regard to land, agrarian laws declare the equal right to land for both sexes. Regardless, in practice many more men own land than women, as well as participate in cooperatives and hold managerial positions in cooperatives. This is again due to patriarchal norms and beliefs that recognize and value men's work in agriculture, while undervaluing the role of women.

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND UPGRADE PROPOSAL

The women participation in the Cuban agricultural sector (agriculture, livestock and forestry) included according to the GNEE has been between 12% up to 18% in 2020. In 2015 were employed 898,500 total workers in this sector, 140,300 of them were women; in 2020 there are 792,000 employed, 137,900 of then are women.

In the cooperatives, the female representation is 15-20%, depending on the type of entity, and the female users reach 11%. The greatest increase in the number of women has been in the occupations of medium level technicians (4.8 per cent), 3.0 per cent in higher-level cooperatives and 6.7 per cent in middle-level technicians.

Recognizing this disbalance, promoting gender equality is a goal established by the Ministry of Agriculture, as described in the Cuban Gender Strategy for Agriculture (described further below).

At the institutional level there is evidence of increasing strategic thinking in regard to gender mainstreaming in key resources sectors, whereby the National System of Small Farmers (ANAP), the Cuban Association of Animal Production (ACPA) and the Association of Agricultural and Forestry Technicians (ACTAF) all have Gender Strategies in place, though in implementation they are still at an early stage. The Ministry of Agriculture (MINAGRI) has also developed a Gender Strategy for its own organization.

Similarly, development projects, driven by increasing international cooperation, have deployed gender equality as one of their main objectives. For example, the project GEA (Gender Equality for Food Security Quality Management), regional experience that has been applied in Cuba through the UNDP/FEU Project “Agricultural Modernization in Cuba (PALMA)” and the UNICEF/COSADE Project “Environmental Bases for Food Security (BASAL).”

Likewise, after a two-year assessment and with the aim of reducing the gaps that lead to inequalities between rural men and women in Cuba, the Ministry of Agriculture (MINAGRI) approved the National Strategy for Gender Equality in Agriculture, Livestock and Forestry, state, budgetary and cooperative institutions, organizations and entities in the period 2015-2020. The Strategy’s mission is: “To guide, encourage and support management for equal rights and opportunities between men and women.” The strategy has four main thematic areas on: recognizing the disadvantages and capacities of women, as well as the needs, problems and potentialsities of each territory; to close gender gaps; and to promote the incorporation, participation and leadership of women in the agricultural, forestry and tobacco sectors (MINAGRI, 2016).

The specific of the Cuban Agriculture Gender Strategy objectives are:
- Generate a management culture for gender equality throughout the Agricultural System.

- To articulate the work for gender equality of the organizations that make up the Agricultural System.

### Strategic Lines:

1. Creation and/or strengthening of capacities: awareness raising and training to close gender gaps.
2. Communication, knowledge management, and innovation to enhance gender equality.
3. Articulation of organizations, networking and socio-cultural impact on communities for gender equality in the agricultural, forestry and tobacco sectors.
4. Work-life balance, reproductive health and reconciliation of family and working life with greater gender equality.
5. Impact on institutional management measures to promote gender equality.
6. Recognition, evaluation, and recognition of entities working for gender equality.
7. Participation and representation (social, moral, political and material), leadership and economic empowerment of women.
8. Address masculinities in the agricultural, forestry and tobacco sector, and their impact on gender equality.

### Summary of gender gaps in the agricultural, forestry and tobacco sector:

1. Non-representative participation: the predominance of men in the labour force is observed.
2. Sexual division of labour in production systems: jobs and occupations considered for men are more remunerated than those for women.
3. Gender gaps in income: although women and men receive equal pay for equal work, women's income is lower than men's because they carry out the highest paid jobs.
4. Gender gaps in education: women are considered to have less knowledge than men about climate change adaptation.
5. Gender gaps in access to and control of resources: women use resources less than men do, which affects their productivity.
6. Gender gaps linked to health risks: men are more exposed in productive areas, while women are more at risk than men in the home.

Regarding the fisheries sector, the fisheries law is defined as inclusive and does not have any de jure gender discrimination. It establishes the regulations for the adequate organization, administration and control of fishing, in accordance with the conservation and rational use of hydrobiological resources in the maritime, fluvial and lacustrine waters of the Republic of Cuba, which are essential to the food sovereignty of the nation.

It defines the principles of management of fishing uses:

a) Conservation and sustainable use.
b) The precautionary approach.

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## The Implementation of Scientific, Technological and Innovative Criteria; and Protection of Marine, River and Lake Ecosystems

It also establishes the regime of fishing authorizations, the fishing modalities, the fishing gear that can be used and the fishing areas. Article 8.1 establishes that no authorization is required for fishing carried out freely by natural persons, whether national or foreign, in maritime, river and lake waters from the coast or natural shores using pots, nets, ropes and hooks, without the use of fish traps, gill nets, purse seine nets, or other gear. However, given its high environmental and historical-cultural importance and resources of state interest, this practice is not permitted without prior authorization.

Regardless, when it comes to participation in the fisheries sector, there is a gendered division of labour within communities, with women having less decision-making regarding Women's roles in supply chains.

At the community level, field observation and interviews with directors of the Martin Marika in Jicaro (an institution that belongs to the Ministry of Tourism, which offers services of nautical activities, sport fishing and diving for tourism), also demonstrate that there is a traditional division of labour within communities. Women are primarily responsible for household tasks (even if they may be involved in fishing or other income-generating activities) as elsewhere in the world; the ones who have the "double duty: split productive and domestic and hence a significant time burden of unpaid work.

Men are primarily economic providers and although they perform some domestic tasks, such as getting water for cooking or washing, they see themselves as helping women rather than undertaking an equal share. Women's responsibilities include not only those of getting water, buying animals for breeding, agricultural purchases (inputs for production), sales of productions, cutting trees to make charcoal for fuel, collecting wood, house purchases, mowing, building new spaces.

As men mainly perform productive roles, they have more time to satisfy their own needs, for recreation and development. Women, on the other hand, lack time for self-care, recreation and development (management positions, training, participation in events and exchange of experiences), among others; for which reason they are at a disadvantage.

In addition, women - especially housewives - are found to be exposed to greater health risks through systematic exposure to oil and firewood for cooking and through the salination of water.

Some examples found during the field visits are as follows:

- In Playa Caño, women play an important role as heads of family and a factor that affects the inhabitants greatly is the water supply problem, which affects the domestic recharge of these women. Approximately 80% of Caño's population is not native to the area. Many of its inhabitants have moved from other areas in search of better living conditions. The houses that have been abandoned by the inhabitants of Caño currently live in La Güira de Melena.
- In La Coloma, women are considered to be primarily responsible for the household and the family, perform domestic work and are disadvantaged because they are economically dependent on men; men work in the fishing industry and practice artisanal fishing. In Zapatón, 10 of them are heads of families and all of these women are married, and they are equally disadvantaged because they are economically dependent on men. The houses are in precarious conditions after the passage of cyclones, many cook with oil or firewood

### Role of Women in Relevant supply chain in Rural Areas

To further elucidate women's access and control of resources, as well as dynamics around labour and employment, an additional survey was administered to understand women’s role in supply chains relevant to the project, including agriculture, fisheries, use of non-timber forest products, and forestry more broadly.

It was found that in the project areas, women participate in a broad range of agricultural activities, including:

1) Soil preparation and Harvest
   - Applying fertilizers / insecticides / or other inputs
   - Weeding
   - Transformation (peeling, washing, grinding)
2) Selling and Exchange
3) Livestock tending

Some of the activities which were identified as most common among women were:
- Planting, applying fertilizers / insecticides / or other inputs,
- Harvesting produce, as well as contributing to administrative tasks and species breeding.


---
**Page 123**
---

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

Women in the project focus areas stated that they were interested in learning about other types of agricultural activities, and responded favourably to activities including gender training workshops, and those organized to promote women’s employment activities.

Women's participation in the fishing sector was found to be primarily related to the processing of catch, as well in the activities related to the selling and exchange of the catch. They process the catch, clean it, weigh it and pack it for local sale (themselves in their community or those working in fishing facilities or companies [EPICOS] in La Colonia and in Jicaro).

The role of women in the use of wood for energy is a closely related to the production of charcoal, which is sold as fuelwood. As such, it demands a lot of activity conducted by women in companies in their territories. In general, this is an activity that is carried out by men, as a labour-intensive activity that requires significant strength, as well as intense working hours to keep the ovens burning and working. In this coal cycle, women work basically in the production process, while men are responsible for selling it.

In the group of women who do the work in the project communities, women hold management positions in many of the forestry units. It is a profitable activity and puts stimulation in foreign currency into the local economy.

It is worth highlighting the fact that, in Cuba, there is a law which prohibits the felling of mangrove trees (Forest Law 8 of 1958), which is reasonably well enforced. Therefore, the wood used for charcoal production does not come from the mangrove, but rather from invasive species such as casuarina and algarrobo in areas outside of the project’s intervention areas. It was found however that in the project area, some inhabitants debark the mangrove to make a type of leather or to build simple utensils for rudimentary fishing, however this is not on any large scale.

In project areas with mangroves, the communities use honeybees, where women are involved in extracting the honey from the hives and taking care of them. Tannin, which is a dye that is extracted from the red mangrove after it is debarked, is also used for medicinal purposes and in the leather industry, although there do not many women involved.

Regarding the case of production of products such as mangroves and corals, surveys found that in the case of Batánbad, women participate in the cleaning and packaging of sponges. In the Rincón de Guanabo Protected Area, there is a current project aimed at the exploitation of sea grasses for the purpose of speciesing a part of the sea grasses, where the participation of women in harvesting and sorting activities has been noted in order to obtain the primary product from which a pharmaceutical product will be developed.

## Marine and Coastal Ecosystem Services

Marine and coastal ecosystem goods and services play a major and vital role in the well-being and way of life of Cuba's coastal communities. These communities generally interact most


---
**Page 124**
---

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCIAL PROPOSAL

with the coastal marine ecosystems, such as coral ridges, reefs and mangroves, which are directly and indirectly exploited.

Among the main economic and environmental uses related to these ecosystems are commercial and self-consumption fisheries; scientific and educational value, coastal protection, erosion control, spiritual, cultural and recreational value, medicinal and conservation value, and biodiversity habitat, among others.

Coral reef ecosystems have use in many of the above-mentioned areas, except sport fishing, tourism and recreation. They also provide more than half of the benefits of these ecosystems, especially in subsistence and sport fishing. Some components of these coral ecosystems are used at a community level as natural medicine or for ornamental purposes, mainly by women.

The products of the Costa Arena vegetation ecosystems are used to produce charcoal, buds, human food, animal feed, medicinal plants, among others. They also provide coastal protection against the impact of storms and hurricanes or as a source of materials for handicraft production, etc. Oyster farms and capping houses are also work options in these communities in terms of demand and opportunities for women.

## The role of women in national forest enterprises

In Cuba, more than 7,500 women farmers are involved in forest management, representing between 18-19% of the workers in this field (Territorial Delegation of the Ministry of Agriculture, 2014). The presence and participation of women in forestry is found in all types of occupations. However, there has been a decrease over time in the figures for these compared to the men are much lower. They are also represented in all forestry structures at the territorial level, whether in forestry enterprises at the provincial level, in forest management units or in the administration and supervision of forests.

In the case of Cuba, the incorporation of women or not into the forestry sector is not so determined by the access they may have to it; since like men they may work in the sector. There are also other factors why women do not enter the sector: economic lack of motivation due to the low value recognized for their work; little knowledge about the sector and insufficient promotion of it so that women can appreciate the potential it offers for individual, social and professional fulfillment; little promotion of employment options that

In the case of Cuba, the incorporation of women or not into the forestry sector is not so determined by the access they may have to it; since like men they may work in the sector. There are also other factors why women do not enter the sector: economic lack of motivation due to the low value recognized for their work; little knowledge about the sector and insufficient promotion of it so that women can appreciate the potential it offers for individual, social and professional fulfillment; little promotion of employment options that

# ANNEX VIII – Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

can be taken up by women, competition from other areas of work with more attractive jobs; distance from work areas and lack of differentiated attention to women's practical needs in terms of working conditions.

Generally speaking, the role of women in forest leadership and management has increased. There is an increasing number of women in leadership positions in forestry who are involved in managing forests and natural resources. To a lesser extent, they work on field activities or on the ground, such as in forest brigades (brigadas). They are also represented in the Forestry Corps, where they certify and control forestry activities, although this body belongs to MININT.

Women have gradually been incorporated into the country's forestry activity, with notable progress in the productive, managerial and organizational spheres. However, there are still gender gaps that require joint action to eliminate them. Some of these are: Greater training and awareness on gender issues for managers of companies, forestry units and workers in the sector; better representation of women directly involved in forest activities (plantations, species, sawmills, coal plans, experimental stations and others); The existence of a greater number of spaces where the topic is included, such as methodological and practical activities, work meetings, training courses, etc., with the aim of increasing their training in the communities from a gender equity perspective, which may contribute to changes in stereotypes related to the activity, thus favouring the incorporation of women into forestry activities.

In Cuba there exist Non-Agricultural Cooperatives (CNA) and Agricultural Cooperatives, which, according to their type, have three names:

a) Basic Unit of Cooperative Production

b) Agricultural Production Cooperative; and

c) Credit and Service Cooperative.

Again, there are no de jure limitations in women's involvement. The requirements for membership in a cooperative are defined according to the contribution of work and goods to the productive functioning of the cooperative, not according to gender or sexual preference.

Cuban legislation on cooperatives:

Decree No. 362 on Non-Agricultural Cooperatives: "The organization is an organization with economic and social purposes, which is constituted voluntarily on the basis of the contribution of goods and rights and is supported by the work of its members. The general objective of the cooperative is the production of goods and the provision of services through collective management for the satisfaction of the social interest and that of the members."

- Decree-Law No. 365 "On Agricultural Cooperatives" The latter [it] contributes an economic and social organization that is part of the agricultural and forestry production system. Its general objective is the production of goods, fundamentally agricultural and livestock, and their commercialization, as well as the provision of services through collective management for the satisfaction of the social interest emanating from the cooperative.

Women were linked to cooperatives through the structure of the Base Enterprise Unit (UEB), which is integrated into state-run agroforestry enterprises. It is estimated that there are about 40 thousand workers in the forestry sector, 9 thousand of them women. Forty percent of the technical and professional staff are women.

The advances in women's participation in Cuban forestry are relevant when compared to the situation found in other countries or even in others around the planet. Cuba's National Forestry Programme and forestry sector policy explicitly include the issue of gender and update the strategy to achieve equity.

Traditionally, men have dominated forestry and forest sciences. With very few exceptions such as CAFICO, women are not well represented in the forestry sector and have not had the opportunity to access positions of power and influence as a result of gender discrimination. They have not been motivated or encouraged to choose the forestry profession. Women represent only 10% of the total number of workers in Cuba; approximately 40% of the technical and professional staff of the National Forestry Directorate (DNF) of the Ministry of Agriculture and the SEF are women. [Cuba Forest Sector Development Plan]

In Cuba, more than 7,500 rural women are involved in forest management, representing 19% of the workers in this field[2]. The presence and participation of women in forestry is found in all types of occupations, including many that have traditionally been considered as men's, but the figures for these components are still very much low and have not yet recognized.

Before the revolution, female, today they hold positions as directors of private companies, services and forestry units, which contributes to a greater promotion of women among foresters, based on awareness rising work at all levels. An example of this is the wide representation of women in the General Assembly of the Association of Small and Medium Agriculture Business Group (GEAM), among others. The incorporation of women has also had a great increase in more practical forestry work, from reforestation actions and work in nurseries, timber industries, sawmills, coal plants, experimental stations, and others; so it is becoming an ideal job.

Another remunerated activity in which women participate is the production of the cogollo, a product obtained from prophylactic felling and from wood that falls when hurricanes strike. This vegetal fiber is produced in a family activity with a paramount duration of use, and is sold to the forestry company for later delivery to the Tobacco Company of the country, which demands the product to tie the bales of tobacco. Women also work in the production

### Gender Based Violence

In recent decades there has been a worldwide movement denouncing gender-based violence, which affects millions of girls and women around the world.[2] Gender-based violence is defined as any intentional act or omission, based on gender inequalities, which results in the possible or actual undermining of human integrity (physical, sexual, emotional, economic, professional, social, cultural, etc.). In Cuba, women are the victims of a patriarchal culture that legitimizes hegemonic male power. In Cuba, this social violence is expressed through different forms and levels of gender-based social action. National campaigns to eliminate all types of violence against women and girls have been fundamental, as well as prevention actions developed at the community level through awareness-raising processes and training for social actors in prevention and attention to gender-based violence in the community setting.

Cuba signed the landmark Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1980. In 1997, the National Plan of Action for Follow-up to the

- 88.7% think that in Cuba there are specific policies and actions in favour of women (89.3% of women and 86% of men): 4.6% "agree in part", 1.7% "disagree" and 4.3% "don't know" or "have doubts".

### d) Legal and administrative framework for the protection of women and the protection of gender equality

The Constitution of the Republic of Cuba (2019) expresses the policy will to guarantee the protection of women, according Article 43 of Chapter VI on equality states that: "The State guarantees that women shall be offered the same opportunities and possibilities as men, in order to achieve their full participation in the development of the country." The Cuban constitution also states that "the State guarantees the exercise of political rights by women and men alike. (...) The State strives to create conditions conducive to the realisation of the principle of equality."

Accordingly, Cuban labour legislation does not accept any discrimination in terms of pay, promotions, benefits or equal training. The creation of children's circles in 1961 and the system of semi-boarding schools were also decisive elements in the massive incorporation of women into working and social life. Thus, in the 1970s and 1980s in Cuba, important laws were passed to guarantee equality between men and women in all areas of public life, including those derived exclusively from gender status. These legal reforms are expressed in the Maternity Act (1973), the Law on Marriage and Family (1974), the Law on Women's Rights (1975), the Social Security Act (1979), the Labour Code (1984) and the Constitution of 2019. Overall, laws in Cuba prohibit discrimination against women and there are currently no restrictions on their civil status.

# ANNEX VIII - Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

The following is a summary of the laws currently in force that favour gender equity across spheres:

* The Family Code, enacted in 1975 and currently being revised, has supported the strengthening of the family as the fundamental unit of society, together with the growing participation of women in all spheres of life and the equal rights of sons and daughters.

Regarding employment, Act No. 49, of December 28, 1984, established the first Labour Code, with special rules for women's work, such as Chapter VIII, which sets out their conditions of service and protection against discrimination in employment. Therefore, this body of law manifests and recognizes the importance of women's participation in the workplace, their high social function being beneficial and the specific provisions for adolescent and pregnant women. The current Labour Code (Act No. 137, dated December 15, 2016) includes the protection of working women in Chapter IV and their inclusion in the growing non-state sector of the economy.

The rights to Protection, Safety and Hygiene at Work were established by Law No. 13 of February 27, 1977 (and its regulations and attached legislation), which establishes the duty of administrations to create adequate working conditions that benefit women's participation in the work process. Act No. 136 of the Labour Code currently regulates these conditions.

These principles do not preclude women's right to develop their aptitude and ability to occupy complex positions, nor does it prevent them from performing "general" tasks outside of work options that they could perform, which would permit the full exercise of their rights. Concepts and rules were then modified and applied, on the basis that only maternity protection is required for pregnant women in order to protect them against discrimination when performing certain tasks. Today in Cuba, these concepts are perfected and enriched through scientific research, experience, and the practical results of women's work, in order to avoid making distinctions between men and women.

The Social Security Law No. 105 provides guidelines to ensure the maternity of working women and the protection of widows in the event of the death of their spouses.

Maternity leave is a fundamental right that guarantees protection for the woman and her daughter or child must have in the medical work order. The Working Women's Maternity Act and all the relevant articles of the Labour Code and other labour legislation, which are part of the Social Security Law No. 105, were established to establish and that have served over the years to protect the rights that must be guaranteed to women in the labour structure. Under Decree-Law No. 234 of 2003 on the Maternity of Women Workers, parents are entitled to receive a benefit under Chapter II of this law, which refers to social benefits, provides that: "Once the postnatal leave is over as well as the period of breastfeeding that must be guaranteed in order to promote the best development of children, the mother and father may decide which of them will take care of the child, how this

### RESPONSIBILITY DISTRIBUTION AND SOCIAL BENEFIT COMMUNICATION

Responsibility will be distributed until the first year of life and who will receive the social benefit, communicating the decision in writing to the parents or guardian of each parent's workplace. The social custom, however, is that very few men have taken leave.

It is also worth mentioning that one of the strategic axes of Cuba’s Economic and Social Development Plan 2010 is that of “Human Development, Equity and Social Justice.” One of the specific goals in this plan is to ensure equal access for all citizens to their rights and duties as all citizens, with equality, inclusion and social justice expressed in access to opportunities, achievements or results, distribution of the benefits of development and confrontation of discrimination. This includes gender equity, race, ethnicity, sexual orientation, disability, territorial origin, religious belief, age and any other discrimination injurious to human dignity.

### VARIOUS SOCIAL MECHANISMS AND POLICIES

Various social mechanisms have also been created to promote policies in favor of women, such as the National Commission for the Care of Children, Youth and Equal Rights of Women in the National Assembly of People’s Power; the National Centre for Sex Education; the Commissions for Prevention and Social Care and the Commission for Women's Employment. The National Sexual Education Program has fostered gender equity since the entire population has free access to these services.

### THREE IMPORTANT PROGRAMS IMPLEMENTED AND PROMOTED

- **National Sexual Education Program:** To promote healthy, responsible sexuality with equal rights and responsibilities between women and men.
- **Women’s and Family Orientation Houses Programme:** Aimed at carrying out individual and group orientation actions in order to ensure that beneficiaries make the necessary changes in their behavior and attitudes towards the assumption of family roles. The Women's Chairs Program in Cuban Universities is part of this approach, with an emphasis on introducing a gender perspective into undergraduate, graduate and research contributing to the training of future professionals.
- **FMC (Fundación de Mujeres Cubanas):** Created in 1965 from its inception has assumed the fight against all forms of discrimination and violence towards women as well as acting as a driving civil force. Among its most significant actions are: contributions to the solution of real needs of women by creating objective conditions for the relief of domestic burdens and responsibilities; the promotion of programmes for the education, training and professionalization of Cuban family and individual conflicts that hindered the presence of women in social life; and the promotion of programmes for cultural improvement, strengthening and promoting women's involvement in productive activities. The FMC has also contributed to the recognition of their roles from the exclusive role of housewife as well as the incorporation of women into

economic life, especially in sectors which women who until then had been marginalized, such as housewives, domestic workers, and prostitutes.

These actions collectively facilitated women's access to work, promoted women’s involvement in income-generating positions, moved towards non-sexist socialization, and encouraged the presence of men and the entire family unit in these changes, while simultaneously promoting the recognition of women's rights and the protection and respect for women's rights. This led to the revision of discriminatory laws and the enactment of others that affirmed women’s right to personal liberty. For example, the Ministry of Labour and Social Security was established in 1976, which not only promoted women into traditionally male jobs, but also promoted regulations that broadly favoured the incorporation of women, protected their rights and guaranteed equal opportunities and pay. The FMC has been and continues to be a key actor in this process, as it is a public institution that works for the benefit of women and the family and has had a decisive influence during recent years on Cuban legislation contemplating different perspectives of male-female relations in society.

### Results from Stakeholder Consultations

Global experience has shown that women in most countries experience the effects of climate change differently than men, both because they are more vulnerable to environmental changes, in their changing relationships to water resources and regarding disasters. This difference impacts them first from physical, cultural and social factors that often linked with poverty, and are particularly relevant in contexts where poverty is significantly gendered. In the Cuban context however, although women have a higher vulnerability than men to climate change, they and men are guaranteed the same access to social services, as well as baseline income. This is evident in the fact that although many other nations, there is a startling difference in mortality rates between men and women during disasters. For example, in Haiti, women and children have been found to be 14 times more likely to die than men during disasters[2].

In Cuba this situation is inverted, with men’s mortality significantly outweighing those of women during disasters.

This observation was strongly supported through the results of the stakeholder consultations carried out to inform the design of the project. The stakeholder consultations, which explicitly covered gender issues, inquired of both institutional and community participants what specific actions they believed should be taken by women or adapted to climate change in the coastal zone of Cuba. The full report of this community consultation can be found in the Stakeholder Consultation and Engagement Plan (Annex 8). Overall, in relation to the

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

question that consisted of whether climate change affected men and women equally; little perception of differences were evident, neither on the part of the women nor of the men interviewed. All respondents agreed however that people with unfavourable living conditions are the most vulnerable to climate change, with children, the elderly and people with disabilities being particularly at risk as they have limited adaptive capacity. Through a participatory mapping exercise, most community members, as well as representatives from government validated that the settlements closest to the coast were the most vulnerable, including both the men and women in those communities.

Regardless, the consultations made it possible to identify the following problems that disproportionately impact women:

- **Problem 1: Water supply and quality due to saline intrusion.** Additionally, sanitation problems in most homes in the project area. For example, in the Surgideros de Batabánó, the houses are full of black or grey water. Women are the most affected by these problems because they are the most responsible for domestic work in the home.

- **Problem 2: Equity and gender equality in access to resources.** This problem is related to the lack of access to resources such as fishing boats and other equipment, which are owned by the Ministry of Tourism. For example, in the community of Marlin Branch, Marina Marlin, an institution that belongs to the Ministry of Tourism, and which offers services of nautical activities (sport fishing and diving for tourists). As mentioned above, these women are mostly involved in work traditionally considered 'women's work' such as the cleaning of boat cabins, and work in the office as secretaries and receptionists. From a gender analysis perspective, this indicates a reproduction of the traditional roles of women in society.

In the community of Jicaro, the houses are located on the beach, with no access to water supply or sanitation services. The children's circle is state or private institutions; in the latter case self-employed, which provides care for pre-school children (1-5 years).

### Project design and implementation

Despite the high level of gender equality in Cuba it has been recognized that women play an important role as heads of families and also have an active participation in community work, and hence this proposal seeks to enhance their role in these spaces, considering them key subjects. The communities where the project will be implemented are disproportionately impacted by the lack of water at the household level, informed the decision to include waste management and sanitation activities as a part of the project (through the Jicaro Community).

The project design also considers lessons learned and successful gender mainstreaming strategies from other projects. In the case of the UNDP/Adaptation Fund Manglar Víbora Project, there was a very positive change in thinking that led many women to become involved in activities that previously only men took on. The same trend is as a positive example the fact that women entered the forest brigades, the forest ranger corps, or in


---

---

# ANNEX VIII - Gender Analysis and Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

management positions at the territorial CITMA. As a result, men were forced to take on household chores for support, and not only do women form part of the kitchen or cleaning staff in the forest brigades, but they also have active roles in these spaces, as forest rangers, which is the regulatory authority for ensuring compliance with forest regulations.

The experiences of other projects in Cuba show that many participate in training and education activities. Some women are seen as an excellent capacity to coordinate projects that contribute to their empowerment and leadership. On the other hand, in Cuba, it is women who are primarily involved in community activities.

The UNDP/FA project Manglar Vivo worked on accompanying the family in many of the activities, especially the mothers who participated for example, in the collection of material for the construction of a house. The project also supported the women for the mangrove ecosystem's defence, national day of mangroves in Cuba, community fairs, educational-communication workshops in schools, and the work of the universities (training of students). In this project, in spite of the fact that they were not officially part of the project team, they participated in planning processes and, although some of them ceased to be an official part of the project because of the household's occupations, they found an alternative to be volunteers within the project.

"At the beginning, when I was proposed, I said to myself: 'Forester?... I am an engineer, but my specialty is not forestry. I don't know anything about it.' But then I started to think and try it. I feel good; I don't have any complexities, even not when sometimes they say to me: do you feel sorry for walking around in boots, with those clothes? The environment in which I work requires a certain type of clothing (Délices, guardian of the forest, UNDP/FA Living Mangrove Project).

"I spend more time in the protected area than I do at home. Sometimes we stay for several days, it's the whole day, we don't have time. Usually we're here, it's our house. My family has supported me a lot and my husband is very proud of what I'm doing. My daughters and grandchildren have been very attached to my work. I even have a granddaughter who is 10 years old and she tells me that she is going to be the future director of Flora and Fauna, because she lives in love with this world in which I work" (Tania, UNDP/FA Living Mangrove Project).

"I have always said, once I discussed it with him, that if I ever ceased to exist I would want to be a red mangrove. Because I identify with him: he has all his family on him when he has to let go, because he is the one who stays in the house and he is the one who takes care of the roots in the ground, you as long as there are winds, tides, cyclones and he is still holding there to the earth. I would like to be a red mangrove" (Tamila, UNDP/FA Living Mangrove Project).

During the visits to the territories and in exchanges with the women, they expressed satisfaction with the implementation of the project in their communities and were eager to participate in future projects. The project team also tried to facilitate their participation in the project activities.

Overall, the design and implementation of the project took into consideration the following gender implications:

- Specific strategies to include/address female-headed households;
- Different concerns and perspectives faced by women;
- A more transparent and data-based identification of gaps in gender equality through the use of sex-disaggregated data in the monitoring of implementation results, and hold individuals and institutions accountable for results that promote gender equality;

## Gender Consideration in Design of Grievance Response Mechanism

As part of the State's political will to achieve gender equality, conditions have been created to support work on caring for and preventing gender violence through:

a) signature and commitment to the CEDAW Convention and other international agreements,

b) the establishment of the Government Platform to implement the agreements of the Beijing Platform,

c) The creation in 1997 of the National Group for Attention to and Prevention of Domestic Violence, among others, until the recognition of gender violence as an obstacle to women's full participation in society and the economy;

d) The deployment of numerous action initiatives to combat gender-based violence in various institutions, non-governmental organizations and agencies that are constituted by the State, with a view to ensuring that the measures taken into account for the improvement of the strategy for addressing the problem of gender-based violence.

e) The legal framework adopted by Cuban women, even if there is not yet a special law on violence, equal opportunities in all spheres of society and as a result, the sustained increase in their social participation, act as factors of prevention and assume systems of protection and treatment.

f) The Constitution of the Republic of Cuba endorsed in 2019, under Title V, relating to "Rights, Duties and Guarantees," recognized that “All persons receive the same protection and treatment from the authorities and enjoy the same rights, freedoms and guarantees without any discrimination on grounds of race, color, gender orientation, gender identity.

31

**Page 136**

In Cuba, community prevention work (including violence prevention) is currently governed by Decree-Law 286 of 2011, enacted by the Council of State of the Republic. The Ministry of Labour and Social Security is the agency assigned the function of proposing, directing and controlling the policy approved by the State and the government in terms of prevention, assistance and social work, for which it follows the following:

1. To propose guidelines and control the procedures for the development of prevention, assistance and social work.
2. To design and guide the characterization of family nuclei and communities; and propose policies for the solution of community problems.
3. To promote actions to act on the solution of social problems, to identify their causes and conditions and to make recommendations to the relevant institutions for appropriate action.
4. Control, direct and coordinate with the corresponding bodies, agencies, organizations and institutions the ways and methods for the integral attention of social problems and the prevention of antisocial and criminal behaviours.
5. To promote research and studies and research to identify causes and conditions of antisocial and criminal behaviour, and to use their results in prevention, assistance and social work.
6. Promote continuous training of the personnel linked to these activities.
7. Coordinate, evaluate and propose strategies for dissemination in accordance with the principles of our society and in support of prevention, assistance and social work.
8. Any other action that is deemed necessary.


Local and community maintenance mechanisms[201]

In the Cuban case, a predominant role has been played by the popular organizations, together with other state bodies and institutions with which they have harmoniously developed integral actions at the community level. Complaints to the directorates of the National Revolutionary Police (PNR) are also received.

However, there are several agents of community social control to which victims of violence can turn, such as "social and mass organizations", among them: The Committee for the Defense of the Revolution (CDR), the Federation of Cuban Women (FMC), the integral manager of the National Revolutionary Police (PNR), the sector chief of the National Revolutionary Police (PNR), the delegate of the People's Power who represents the inhabitants before the corresponding municipal assembly.

- Greater dissemination and access to existing mechanisms;
- Greater visibility (print media, audio visual) and understanding of gender violence, which often overlaps. In the popular imagination it is only perceived as physical and

### Monitoring and evaluation

During project implementation, qualitative assessments will be made of gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated with the project. This will be incorporated into the Annual Project Implementation Report, the Mid-Term Evaluation and the Final Evaluation.
Indicators to quantify the achievement of project objectives in relation to gender equality will include:
- Increase in women's participation from community-based conservation solutions
- Number of men and women employed in jobs created by the project
- Training opportunities, knowledge management and information dissemination.

More specifically, to monitor and evaluate the progress of the project, the following indicators should be used:

Qualitative results:
* The project can generate employment for women from the actions foreseen in the interventions in the forestry and water resources sector, as well as in the organizational structures associated for the implementation of the project.
* Save time for women as a result of shorter working hours required for agricultural and water resource management activities. This will allow them to participate more actively and encourage greater co-participation of men in domestic tasks, through the project's sensitization and capacity building activities.
* Support training and education activities that may include activities related to climate change adaptation, fisheries leadership, decision making, thus enabling greater participation of women to participate with confidence in community meetings.
* Empower women through the project’s capacity building and training to facilitate their participation in the project's actions at the community level.
